### Unsupervised Radar Point Cloud Enhancement via Arbitrary LiDAR Guided Diffusion Prior
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09887v1)
- **Authors**: Yanlong Yang, Jianan Liu, Guanxiong Luo, Hao Li, Euijoon Ahn, Mostafa Rahimi Azghadi, Tao Huang
- **Abstract**: In industrial automation, radar is a critical sensor in machine perception. However, the angular resolution of radar is inherently limited by the Rayleigh criterion, which depends on both the radar's operating wavelength and the effective aperture of its antenna array.To overcome these hardware-imposed limitations, recent neural network-based methods have leveraged high-resolution LiDAR data, paired with radar measurements, during training to enhance radar point cloud resolution. While effective, these approaches require extensive paired datasets, which are costly to acquire and prone to calibration error. These challenges motivate the need for methods that can improve radar resolution without relying on paired high-resolution ground-truth data. Here, we introduce an unsupervised radar points enhancement algorithm that employs an arbitrary LiDAR-guided diffusion model as a prior without the need for paired training data. Specifically, our approach formulates radar angle estimation recovery as an inverse problem and incorporates prior knowledge through a diffusion model with arbitrary LiDAR domain knowledge. Experimental results demonstrate that our method attains high fidelity and low noise performance compared to traditional regularization techniques. Additionally, compared to paired training methods, it not only achieves comparable performance but also offers improved generalization capability. To our knowledge, this is the first approach that enhances radar points output by integrating prior knowledge via a diffusion model rather than relying on paired training data. Our code is available at https://github.com/yyxr75/RadarINV.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel unsupervised method for enhancing the angular resolution of radar point clouds.  The key idea is to leverage a diffusion model trained on *arbitrary* LiDAR data as a prior without requiring paired radar-LiDAR training data.  The radar angle estimation recovery is formulated as an inverse problem, and the diffusion model acts as a prior distribution to regularize the solution.  Experiments on RADIal and K-Radar datasets demonstrate that the proposed method achieves comparable or even superior performance to supervised methods, especially in terms of generalization ability, without requiring paired data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's most significant contribution is its unsupervised approach to radar point cloud enhancement using an *arbitrary* LiDAR-guided diffusion model prior. Existing methods primarily rely on supervised learning with paired radar-LiDAR data, which is costly to acquire and prone to calibration errors. By eliminating the need for paired data and using a diffusion model as a prior, the paper introduces a significantly more flexible and practical solution. While diffusion models have been used for radar enhancement before, their application in a truly unsupervised manner, guided by arbitrary LiDAR data, appears novel.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in radar-based perception: the inherent angular resolution constraints. Enhancing radar resolution is vital for various applications, including autonomous driving and industrial automation. This work has the potential to make advanced perception systems more affordable and robust, especially in scenarios where LiDAR data is unavailable or unreliable. The superior generalization capability, as demonstrated by the cross-dataset validation, is also a significant advantage.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Unsupervised Approach:** Addresses the limitations of paired radar-LiDAR training data.
    *   **Strong Generalization:**  Demonstrated through cross-dataset validation.
    *   **Good Performance:** Achieves competitive results compared to supervised methods.
    *   **Clear Formulation:** Formulates radar enhancement as an inverse problem within a Bayesian framework.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are known to be computationally expensive, which can limit their applicability in real-time systems. The paper acknowledges this in future work section and provides an analysis in the appendix and offers solutions for acceleration, which are quite preliminary.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** More thorough ablation studies exploring the sensitivity of performance to different diffusion model architectures or LiDAR data characteristics (e.g., density, resolution) could be included.
    * **Dependency on Prior Quality**: Performance is directly related to the quality of the LiDAR-based prior (diffusion model). Poorly trained or biased priors will negatively impact the enhanced radar data. The paper should explicitly acknowledge this.
* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of radar-based perception research. It opens up new possibilities for developing more flexible and practical perception systems by decoupling radar and LiDAR data requirements. While it is unlikely to immediately replace existing methods, it provides a promising alternative, particularly for applications with limited resources or strict cost constraints.
* **Rigor:** The paper is well-written and the claims are supported by experimental results. The methods are clearly explained and the evaluation metrics are appropriate. The comparisons to other methods are also fair and relevant.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper offers a novel and significant contribution to the field by proposing an unsupervised approach, the high computational cost presents a barrier to immediate widespread adoption. The lack of ablation studies also restricts the ability to fully understand the solution. Therefore, I would rate this as a **solid contribution** to the field, that while still has space to improve, can shift the current paradigm.

### Diffusion-SAFE: Shared Autonomy Framework with Diffusion for Safe Human-to-Robot Driving Handover
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09889v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxin Fan, Monroe Kennedy III
- **Abstract**: Safe handover in shared autonomy for vehicle control is well-established in modern vehicles. However, avoiding accidents often requires action several seconds in advance. This necessitates understanding human driver behavior and an expert control strategy for seamless intervention when a collision or unsafe state is predicted. We propose Diffusion-SAFE, a closed-loop shared autonomy framework leveraging diffusion models to: (1) predict human driving behavior for detection of potential risks, (2) generate safe expert trajectories, and (3) enable smooth handovers by blending human and expert policies over a short time horizon. Unlike prior works which use engineered score functions to rate driving performance, our approach enables both performance evaluation and optimal action sequence generation from demonstrations. By adjusting the forward and reverse processes of the diffusion-based copilot, our method ensures a gradual transition of control authority, by mimicking the drivers' behavior before intervention, which mitigates abrupt takeovers, leading to smooth transitions. We evaluated Diffusion-SAFE in both simulation (CarRacing-v0) and real-world (ROS-based race car), measuring human-driving similarity, safety, and computational efficiency. Results demonstrate a 98.5\% successful handover rate, highlighting the framework's effectiveness in progressively correcting human actions and continuously sampling optimal robot actions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Diffusion-SAFE: Shared Autonomy Framework with Diffusion for Safe Human-to-Robot Driving Handover" introduces a novel closed-loop shared autonomy framework designed to improve safety during human-to-robot driving handovers. The framework, Diffusion-SAFE, uses two diffusion models: an evaluator to predict human driving intent and identify potential risks, and a copilot to generate safe expert trajectories.  It modulates the forward and reverse processes of the copilot diffusion model to seamlessly blend human and expert policies, mitigating abrupt takeovers. The framework is evaluated in both simulation and real-world (ROS-based race car) environments, measuring human-driving similarity, safety, and computational efficiency. The results demonstrate a high success rate in safe handovers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The use of diffusion models in a shared autonomy context, particularly for driving handover, demonstrates a good degree of novelty. While diffusion models are gaining traction in robotics and motion planning, their application to *shared* autonomy with emphasis on smooth transitions is a relative contribution.  The modulation of forward/reverse diffusion processes for control transfer is also a unique aspect. However, the core ideas of using prediction and planning for safe handover aren't entirely new. The novelty lies in the *implementation* via diffusion models and the control transfer mechanism.

* **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in autonomous driving: ensuring safe and seamless transitions between human and robot control.  Achieving safe handovers is essential for building trust in autonomous systems and mitigating potential accidents. If the reported performance (98.5% handover success) generalizes, it represents a significant advancement in this domain.
The framework's ability to learn from demonstrations rather than relying on hand-tuned reward functions offers several benefits, including increased adaptability and robustness. However, the significance is somewhat limited by the simplicity of the CarRacing-v0 environment, and the controlled conditions of the real-world experiments. More robust real-world testing, with a more complex driving environment, is necessary to demonstrate the broader applicability of the method.

* **Strengths:**
    *   The integration of diffusion models for both prediction and planning is a strong point, providing a coherent framework.
    *   The control transfer mechanism via modulating diffusion processes offers a promising approach to smooth handovers.
    *   The empirical evaluation in both simulation and a real-world setting provides initial evidence of the framework's effectiveness.
    *   The clear articulation of the problem and the proposed solution, along with a well-defined experimental setup, enhances the paper's clarity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The real-world evaluation, while present, is limited in scope. The environment is simplified (fixed track, known obstacles), which doesn't fully capture the complexities of real-world driving scenarios.
    *   The reliance on a motion capture system for real-world experiments restricts the deployability of the framework in its current form. A camera based solution would be better.
    *   The paper could benefit from more detailed analysis of the computational cost and scalability of the diffusion models, especially for real-time applications.
    *   The baseline comparisons, while included, could be more comprehensive.

* **Potential Impact:** If the framework's performance can be replicated in more complex and realistic driving environments, it could significantly impact the development of shared autonomy systems. The diffusion-based approach offers a promising direction for handling uncertainty and enabling smooth human-robot collaboration. The approach might also be transferable to other robotic domains where shared autonomy and safe control transitions are critical.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its strengths, Diffusion-SAFE has some limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than revolutionary. The real-world validation, while encouraging, is somewhat constrained. Therefore, while the framework presents a valuable contribution with promising results, the need for more extensive validation and demonstration in realistic environments is necessary to achieve a greater score.

### Comparing Exploration-Exploitation Strategies of LLMs and Humans: Insights from Standard Multi-armed Bandit Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09901v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyuan Zhang, Darcy Wang, Ningyuan Chen, Rodrigo Mansur, Vahid Sarhangian
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate or automate human behavior in complex sequential decision-making tasks. A natural question is then whether LLMs exhibit similar decision-making behavior to humans, and can achieve comparable (or superior) performance. In this work, we focus on the exploration-exploitation (E&E) tradeoff, a fundamental aspect of dynamic decision-making under uncertainty. We employ canonical multi-armed bandit (MAB) tasks introduced in the cognitive science and psychiatry literature to conduct a comparative study of the E&E strategies of LLMs, humans, and MAB algorithms. We use interpretable choice models to capture the E&E strategies of the agents and investigate how explicit reasoning, through both prompting strategies and reasoning-enhanced models, shapes LLM decision-making. We find that reasoning shifts LLMs toward more human-like behavior, characterized by a mix of random and directed exploration. In simple stationary tasks, reasoning-enabled LLMs exhibit similar levels of random and directed exploration compared to humans. However, in more complex, non-stationary environments, LLMs struggle to match human adaptability, particularly in effective directed exploration, despite achieving similar regret in certain scenarios. Our findings highlight both the promise and limits of LLMs as simulators of human behavior and tools for automated decision-making and point to potential areas of improvements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper compares the exploration-exploitation (E&E) strategies of large language models (LLMs) to those of humans and traditional multi-armed bandit (MAB) algorithms. The study uses standard MAB tasks from cognitive science and psychiatry, analyzing the decisions made by commercial LLMs, human participants, and MAB algorithms. The authors investigate how reasoning, facilitated by prompting and reasoning-enhanced models, influences LLM decision-making. The results show that reasoning shifts LLMs towards more human-like behavior, characterized by both random and directed exploration. While LLMs can achieve similar levels of E&E to humans in simple stationary tasks, they struggle to match human adaptability, particularly in directed exploration, in more complex non-stationary environments. Despite similar regret in some scenarios, these limitations affect the efficiency and performance. The paper highlights the potential and limitations of LLMs as human behavior simulators and automated decision-making tools.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Comparative Study:**  The paper provides a well-structured comparative analysis of LLMs, human participants, and classic MAB algorithms. The comparison across these three distinct agent types provides a strong foundation for understanding the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in the context of sequential decision-making under uncertainty.
*   **Focus on Exploration-Exploitation (E&E):** The E&E framework is fundamental to dynamic decision-making, and the paper rightly identifies it as a crucial aspect for evaluating how well LLMs can simulate human behavior in uncertain tasks. This narrow focus provides a depth of insight absent in many broader AI behavior analyses.
*   **Use of Human Data and Real-World Tasks:** By leveraging datasets from actual human MAB task experiments, the paper grounds the LLM performance evaluation in empirical human behavior. This approach is more convincing than simply comparing LLMs against simulated or algorithmic benchmarks.
*   **Analysis of Reasoning's Impact:** The paper's investigation of how reasoning via prompting ("Chain-of-Thought") and reasoning-enhanced architectures influences LLM E&E is an important contribution. The finding that reasoning helps shift LLMs towards more human-like behavior is significant.
*   **Clear Performance Metrics and Interpretability:** The use of model-based metrics to capture the exploration-exploitation trade-off (random and directed exploration) is a strength, as it provides a more nuanced understanding than simple performance comparisons. The use of well-established choice models allows for parameter comparison to prior human data.
*   **Well-defined Limitations:** A major strength is that the paper is frank about the limitations of the approach and identifies clear avenues for future research. The discussions on alignment with human E&E strategies, limited demographic data, and the current focus on commercial LLMs create a roadmap for future investigations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Complexity:** While the 4-armed bandit task is more complex than a 2-armed task, both are still relatively simple compared to real-world sequential decision-making problems.  The complexity of decision-making environments has been shown to dramatically affect LLM performance and decision-making patterns.
*   **Scope of Reasoning:** The reasoning prompts used are relatively simple, and the paper explicitly notes that it's not mathematical or step-by-step reasoning. More sophisticated reasoning, potentially leveraging tools or external knowledge, might reveal further insights into LLM decision-making.
*   **Model Selection Justification:** While the choice models used are justified based on prior literature and good predictive performance, the approach to model selection is less rigorous. Different choice models have significantly different inductive biases. There is little justification for using different choice models for LLMs versus humans or 2-armed versus 4-armed bandit tasks.
*   **Limited Number of LLMs Used:** The paper focuses on a relatively small set of commercial LLMs. More comprehensive testing across a wider range of models, including open-source and specialized LLMs, could strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Lack of Mechanistic Explanation:** The paper identifies differences in E&E strategies but offers limited mechanistic explanations for *why* these differences occur. Speculation about LLM architecture or training data influence is lacking.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the systematic comparison of LLMs, humans, and MAB algorithms on sequential decision-making tasks using the E&E framework *and* employing human data.  The finding that reasoning can improve LLMs' exploration strategies and reduce regret is a significant result with practical implications for using LLMs in automated decision-making. The paper moves beyond simple performance comparisons to provide insights into the underlying decision processes.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has strengths in its comprehensive methodology, real-world dataset, focus on reasoning, and clear articulation of limitations, it also suffers from simplifications in the choice models and scope of tasks. The impact is somewhat limited by the fairly specific E&E framework. Nonetheless, the paper provides important insights to the comparative analysis of human and LLM E&E performance, providing good practical insights and future work.

### Crossing Borders Without Crossing Boundaries: How Sociolinguistic Awareness Can Optimize User Engagement with Localized Spanish AI Models Across Hispanophone Countries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09902v1)
- **Authors**: Martin Capdevila, Esteban Villa Turek, Ellen Karina Chumbe Fernandez, Luis Felipe Polo Galvez, Luis Cadavid, Andrea Marroquin, Rebeca Vargas Quesada, Johanna Crew, Nicole Vallejo Galarraga, Christopher Rodriguez, Diego Gutierrez, Radhi Datla
- **Abstract**: Large language models are, by definition, based on language. In an effort to underscore the critical need for regional localized models, this paper examines primary differences between variants of written Spanish across Latin America and Spain, with an in-depth sociocultural and linguistic contextualization therein. We argue that these differences effectively constitute significant gaps in the quotidian use of Spanish among dialectal groups by creating sociolinguistic dissonances, to the extent that locale-sensitive AI models would play a pivotal role in bridging these divides. In doing so, this approach informs better and more efficient localization strategies that also serve to more adequately meet inclusivity goals, while securing sustainable active daily user growth in a major low-risk investment geographic area. Therefore, implementing at least the proposed five sub variants of Spanish addresses two lines of action: to foment user trust and reliance on AI language models while also demonstrating a level of cultural, historical, and sociolinguistic awareness that reflects positively on any internationalization strategy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper argues for the importance of sociolinguistic awareness in developing localized Spanish AI models across Hispanophone countries. It highlights the significant regional variations in written Spanish across Latin America and Spain, emphasizing that a one-size-fits-all approach can lead to sociolinguistic dissonance and hinder user engagement. The paper proposes implementing at least five sub-variants of Spanish to better cater to regional differences, aiming to increase user trust, reliance on AI models, and overall user experience. The paper discusses several factors that contribute to these variations, including Indigenous language influences, colloquial and standardized grammatical norms, lexical differences, and pop culture influences (specifically reggaeton). It provides examples of grammatical variations, regional lexical differences, slang, and diminutive usage, illustrating the need for nuanced localization. The paper also proposes a method for grouping dialects into five core variants based on geographical areas, grammatical usage, and linguistic exchange. Finally, it advocates for localization as a driver of user experience, emphasizing that adapting AI models to local markets is crucial for product adoption, engagement, and overall success.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a compelling case for the localization of Spanish AI models, emphasizing the crucial role of sociolinguistic awareness in enhancing user experience and fostering inclusivity. While the general argument is sound, the paper's novelty and significance within the field require careful scrutiny.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Articulated Argument:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of linguistic homogenization and the potential benefits of localization.
*   **Comprehensive Overview:** It provides a broad overview of the key factors contributing to Spanish dialectal variations, including Indigenous language influences, lexical differences, grammatical variations, and pop culture.
*   **Concrete Examples:** The paper uses specific examples to illustrate the nuances of Spanish dialects, making the argument more accessible and persuasive.
*   **Practical Recommendations:** The proposal of five core variant groupings provides a starting point for practical implementation.
*   **Emphasis on Inclusivity:**  The argument connects localization to inclusivity, a currently highly relevant goal for AI development.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The core argument – that language models should be localized – is not entirely new. While the specific application to Spanish and the emphasis on sociolinguistic nuances are valuable, the fundamental idea has been previously explored in other contexts and language settings. The literature review references some of these attempts. The paper needs to highlight much better the **specific** contribution of it, in comparison to these other attempts.
*   **Oversimplification of Dialectal Groupings:** The proposed five core variant groupings, while providing a framework, are arguably oversimplified.  The paper acknowledges this somewhat, but does not fully address the significant intra-group variations.  Defining clear boundaries is difficult, and the paper could benefit from a more nuanced discussion of this challenge.  The presented maps and previous studies, including the one from Tellez et al. (2021), show the limitations of geographical categorizations.  The paper's method does not provide an improvement, nor it offers an innovative approach to deal with the problem of regionalization.
*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper lacks empirical evidence to support its claims. While it cites studies on linguistic variations and the success of localization strategies in other contexts, it does not present any original data or experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed five-variant approach. While the paper is written as a *strategy report*, it's impact and novelty will greatly improve if it provides supporting evidence.
*   **Limited Discussion of Implementation Challenges:** The paper touches on practical implementation, but it could benefit from a deeper discussion of the challenges involved in developing and maintaining localized AI models, such as data collection, model training, and ongoing adaptation to linguistic evolution.
*   **Lack of Discussion about Computational Resources and Scalability** How computationally expensive would this approach be? Would this method scalable to new linguistic scenarios or models?

**Significance:**

Despite its limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution by raising awareness of the importance of sociolinguistic awareness in AI development. It highlights the potential pitfalls of linguistic homogenization and advocates for a more nuanced and inclusive approach. The paper provides a useful overview of Spanish dialectal variations and offers a practical framework for localization that could serve as a starting point for future research and development efforts.

**Score:**

**Score: 6/10**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-articulated and persuasive argument for the localization of Spanish AI models, drawing on established linguistic principles and providing concrete examples. However, the core argument lacks significant novelty, and the proposed five-variant approach is arguably oversimplified. The absence of empirical validation and the limited discussion of implementation challenges weaken the paper's overall impact. While the paper makes a valuable contribution by raising awareness of the issue and providing a practical framework, its limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. Its importance is somewhat diminished by the fact that its arguments is mostly derivative from the current literature on localization, with a limited amount of original content.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### UICopilot: Automating UI Synthesis via Hierarchical Code Generation from Webpage Designs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09904v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Gui, Yao Wan, Zhen Li, Zhongyi Zhang, Dongping Chen, Hongyu Zhang, Yi Su, Bohua Chen, Xing Zhou, Wenbin Jiang, Xiangliang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Automating the synthesis of User Interfaces (UIs) plays a crucial role in enhancing productivity and accelerating the development lifecycle, reducing both development time and manual effort. Recently, the rapid development of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has made it possible to generate front-end Hypertext Markup Language (HTML) code directly from webpage designs. However, real-world webpages encompass not only a diverse array of HTML tags but also complex stylesheets, resulting in significantly lengthy code. The lengthy code poses challenges for the performance and efficiency of MLLMs, especially in capturing the structural information of UI designs. To address these challenges, this paper proposes UICopilot, a novel approach to automating UI synthesis via hierarchical code generation from webpage designs. The core idea of UICopilot is to decompose the generation process into two stages: first, generating the coarse-grained HTML hierarchical structure, followed by the generation of fine-grained code. To validate the effectiveness of UICopilot, we conduct experiments on a real-world dataset, i.e., WebCode2M. Experimental results demonstrate that UICopilot significantly outperforms existing baselines in both automatic evaluation metrics and human evaluations. Specifically, statistical analysis reveals that the majority of human annotators prefer the webpages generated by UICopilot over those produced by GPT-4V.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "UICOPILOT: Automating UI Synthesis via Hierarchical Code Generation from Webpage Designs" proposes a novel two-stage approach to automatically generate UI code (HTML/CSS) from webpage designs (screenshots). The approach addresses the challenges of lengthy code generation and complex nested structures in real-world webpages, which limit the performance of existing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). UICOPILOT first generates a coarse-grained HTML hierarchical structure using a Vision Transformer (ViT)-based model, predicting node types, hierarchy, and bounding boxes (BBoxes). Second, it generates fine-grained HTML/CSS code for each subregion of the image based on the DOM tree predicted. The final step is to refine the global code using GPT-4V. Experiments on the WebCode2M dataset demonstrate that UICOPILOT significantly outperforms several baselines in both automatic evaluation metrics (CLIP, SSIM, visual score) and human evaluations, showing improved code fidelity to the original webpage design. The paper underscores the effectiveness of decoupling structure parsing from fine-grained code generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its hierarchical decomposition of the UI code generation task. While using MLLMs for code generation is not new, the specific two-stage approach that first constructs a coarse DOM tree and then generates fine-grained code is a valuable contribution. The BBox-based data pruning to improve the performance of the structure model is also novel. The combination of a ViT-based structural model and GPT-4V in a cascaded manner is a practical application of existing technologies to address a specific problem.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its practical implications for front-end development automation. Automating UI synthesis reduces development time and minimizes errors. Addressing the limitations of one-step code generation approaches for real-world webpages is crucial. The experiments clearly show that UICOPILOT generates visually more accurate webpages than GPT-4V alone, evidenced by automatic metrics and human evaluation. This demonstrates an advancement toward effective UI code generation. The paper addresses limitations of current MLLMs on complex, real-world web pages.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The two-stage hierarchical approach is well-motivated and demonstrated to be effective in handling complex webpages.
    *   The use of BBox-based data pruning improves model training and performance.
    *   Extensive experiments on a large real-world dataset (WebCode2M) provide strong empirical support.
    *   The comparison with several state-of-the-art baselines, including fine-tuned and general-purpose MLLMs, is thorough.
    *   Human evaluation provides valuable insights and complements the automatic metrics.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on GPT-4V: While the architecture outperforms standalone GPT-4V, it remains heavily reliant on a proprietary model. Exploring open-source models for the fine-grained code generation would strengthen the contribution and improve reproducibility.
    *   Limited Scope: The paper focuses on static webpages (HTML/CSS) and excludes JavaScript. Addressing the generation of dynamic website behaviors would increase the impact.
    *  While the paper shows significant improvements in generating visual aspects of web pages, there isn't a thorough study on the accessibility of generated web pages compared with a baseline or the original.
*   **Potential Impact:**  The work has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and accurate UI code generation tools. The hierarchical approach could be adopted and extended in other code generation scenarios.  Future research may build upon this strategy by incorporating JavaScript or focusing on improved domain-specific data pre-processing techniques. It will likely attract attention from both academic and industrial researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods for automating UI code generation by decomposing the task into a hierarchical structure. The approach is empirically validated, and the results are convincing. However, the dependence on a proprietary MLLM (GPT-4V) and the limited scope of static webpages prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The innovation is solid, but the reliance on closed-source technology is a major limitation.

### PIG: Privacy Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Gradient-based Iterative In-Context Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09921v1)
- **Authors**: Yidan Wang, Yanan Cao, Yubing Ren, Fang Fang, Zheng Lin, Binxing Fang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in various domains but pose inherent privacy risks. Existing methods to evaluate privacy leakage in LLMs often use memorized prefixes or simple instructions to extract data, both of which well-alignment models can easily block. Meanwhile, Jailbreak attacks bypass LLM safety mechanisms to generate harmful content, but their role in privacy scenarios remains underexplored. In this paper, we examine the effectiveness of jailbreak attacks in extracting sensitive information, bridging privacy leakage and jailbreak attacks in LLMs. Moreover, we propose PIG, a novel framework targeting Personally Identifiable Information (PII) and addressing the limitations of current jailbreak methods. Specifically, PIG identifies PII entities and their types in privacy queries, uses in-context learning to build a privacy context, and iteratively updates it with three gradient-based strategies to elicit target PII. We evaluate PIG and existing jailbreak methods using two privacy-related datasets. Experiments on four white-box and two black-box LLMs show that PIG outperforms baseline methods and achieves state-of-the-art (SoTA) results. The results underscore significant privacy risks in LLMs, emphasizing the need for stronger safeguards. Our code is availble at \href{https://github.com/redwyd/PrivacyJailbreak}{https://github.com/redwyd/PrivacyJailbreak}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PIG: Privacy Jailbreak Attack on LLMs via Gradient-based Iterative In-Context Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PIG, a novel framework designed to enhance privacy jailbreak attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the shortcomings of existing methods by leveraging in-context learning (ICL) and gradient-based optimization techniques to extract Personally Identifiable Information (PII). PIG identifies PII entities and types, builds a privacy context using ICL, and iteratively refines the context through three gradient-based strategies to elicit sensitive data. The framework is evaluated on both white-box and black-box LLMs using two privacy-related datasets, demonstrating improved performance over existing jailbreak methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of gradient-based iterative optimization *specifically tailored* for privacy jailbreak attacks, building upon the concept of using jailbreak attacks for privacy leakage. While jailbreak attacks and data extraction from LLMs are not entirely new, the approach of iteratively optimizing the in-context learning context for PII extraction is a distinctive contribution. The combination of random, entity-focused, and dynamic token optimization strategies offers a more nuanced approach than simply relying on predefined templates or random search.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights the crucial yet often overlooked aspect of privacy risks in LLMs. It demonstrates that even well-aligned models are vulnerable to carefully crafted jailbreak attacks specifically designed to extract PII. The improved attack success rate of PIG compared to existing methods underscores the seriousness of the privacy risk and emphasizes the need for stronger safeguards. The exploration of privacy leakage from both training data (memorization) and inference context (ICL) provides a comprehensive view of potential attack vectors. The results suggest that LLMs need to be specifically hardened against these kinds of attacks, which might involve different techniques than typical safety alignment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem and Approach:** The paper clearly defines the problem of privacy jailbreak attacks and proposes a structured framework to address it.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The framework is thoroughly evaluated on various LLMs (white-box and black-box) and datasets, offering strong empirical evidence for its effectiveness.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of different components of the PIG framework, allowing for a better understanding of its inner workings.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, experimental setup, and results.
    *   **Focus on an under-explored area:** The authors explicitly bridge the gap between jailbreak attacks and privacy leakage, demonstrating that jailbreak methods can be adapted and optimized for privacy-related scenarios.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Warning:** The warning given about 'offensive model outputs' is concerning. The paper claims that privacy attacks benefit from randomly synthesizing PII, but fails to state how/whether this PII is checked to avoid being offensive/biased.
    *   **Limited Real-World Scenarios:** While the datasets are relevant, the experiments primarily focus on extracting PII from controlled environments (system prompts or memorized data). More real-world scenarios, such as attacks targeting user-generated data or data from RAG systems, would strengthen the paper's impact.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The gradient-based iterative optimization can be computationally expensive, limiting its practical applicability for very large models or real-time scenarios. The paper could benefit from a discussion of potential optimizations or trade-offs.
    *   **Defense Strategies:** While the paper focuses on attacks, it would be valuable to include a discussion of potential defense strategies to mitigate privacy jailbreak attacks.
    *   **Transferability:** While the paper mentions transferability, the discussion could be more in-depth, exploring factors influencing the success of transferring attacks across different LLM architectures and alignment strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by raising awareness of privacy risks in LLMs and providing a practical framework for evaluating and mitigating these risks. It could stimulate further research on developing more robust privacy defenses and alignment strategies tailored to prevent PII extraction. The PIG framework itself could serve as a benchmark for evaluating the effectiveness of future privacy attack mitigation techniques.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel approach to a relevant problem, providing significant evidence of its effectiveness. However, the limitations regarding real-world scenarios, computational cost, a relative lack of discussion of corresponding defenses and transferability issues slightly detract from the overall impact.

### Improving the Euclidean Diffusion Generation of Manifold Data by Mitigating Score Function Singularity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09922v1)
- **Authors**: Zichen Liu, Wei Zhang, Tiejun Li
- **Abstract**: Euclidean diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in generative modeling across diverse domains, and they have been extended to manifold case in recent advances. Instead of explicitly utilizing the structure of special manifolds as studied in previous works, we investigate direct sampling of the Euclidean diffusion models for general manifold-constrained data in this paper. We reveal the multiscale singularity of the score function in the embedded space of manifold, which hinders the accuracy of diffusion-generated samples. We then present an elaborate theoretical analysis of the singularity structure of the score function by separating it along the tangential and normal directions of the manifold. To mitigate the singularity and improve the sampling accuracy, we propose two novel methods: (1) Niso-DM, which introduces non-isotropic noise along the normal direction to reduce scale discrepancies, and (2) Tango-DM, which trains only the tangential component of the score function using a tangential-only loss function. Numerical experiments demonstrate that our methods achieve superior performance on distributions over various manifolds with complex geometries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating data constrained to a manifold using Euclidean diffusion models. The core challenge is the singularity of the score function in the embedded Euclidean space. The paper theoretically analyzes this singularity, separating it into tangential and normal components. To mitigate the singularity and improve sample quality, the authors propose two methods: (1) Niso-DM, which uses non-isotropic noise perturbation along the normal direction, and (2) Tango-DM, which trains only the tangential component of the score function.  Experimental results on various manifolds demonstrate the superiority of the proposed methods over baseline diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its detailed theoretical analysis of the score function singularity in Euclidean diffusion models applied to manifold data. Separating the score function into tangential and normal components provides a clearer understanding of the problem.  The proposed Niso-DM and Tango-DM methods, while not radically different from existing diffusion model architectures, are novel in their application to this specific problem and their motivated design based on the theoretical analysis. Tango-DM, in particular, is an interesting approach to simplifying the learning problem. The connection drawn between the embedded Euclidean score and the Riemannian score function is a new contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem. Manifold-constrained data is ubiquitous in scientific and engineering fields, and efficient generative models are crucial.  Directly applying Euclidean diffusion models is appealing due to the available tooling and infrastructure. The identified singularity issue is a known problem, and the paper offers a practically motivated solution supported by theoretical arguments and empirical results.  However, prior work has touched on related problems, so its contribution is incremental rather than revolutionary. The results on complex manifolds like SO(10) are significant.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a rigorous theoretical analysis of the score function singularity and its decomposition.
    * **Well-Motivated Methods:**  The proposed methods are directly derived from the theoretical analysis, making them intuitive and justifiable.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments on diverse and challenging manifold datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    * **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is generally well-written and organized, making the technical details relatively accessible.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Contribution:** While the analysis is novel and the methods perform well, the overall contribution could be seen as incremental. Existing works have already explored similar issues.
    * **Complexity:** The theoretical analysis is mathematically involved, potentially limiting its accessibility to some readers.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The paper hints at hyperparameter sensitivity, potentially reducing ease of use.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on diffusion models for manifold data. The analysis of the score function singularity provides a foundation for developing more robust and efficient methods. The proposed Niso-DM and Tango-DM could become practical tools for practitioners working with manifold-constrained data. The theoretical connections provided may spawn further research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates significant understanding of the problem space, presents a novel theoretical analysis, and implements well-motivated and empirically validated solutions. While the contribution is incremental, it provides valuable insights and practical tools to the community. The paper is theoretically sound and the techniques provided have potential impact.

### From Trade-off to Synergy: A Versatile Symbiotic Watermarking Framework for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09924v1)
- **Authors**: Yidan Wang, Yubing Ren, Yanan Cao, Binxing Fang
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has heightened concerns about the misuse of AI-generated text, making watermarking a promising solution. Mainstream watermarking schemes for LLMs fall into two categories: logits-based and sampling-based. However, current schemes entail trade-offs among robustness, text quality, and security. To mitigate this, we integrate logits-based and sampling-based schemes, harnessing their respective strengths to achieve synergy. In this paper, we propose a versatile symbiotic watermarking framework with three strategies: serial, parallel, and hybrid. The hybrid framework adaptively embeds watermarks using token entropy and semantic entropy, optimizing the balance between detectability, robustness, text quality, and security. Furthermore, we validate our approach through comprehensive experiments on various datasets and models. Experimental results indicate that our method outperforms existing baselines and achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. We believe this framework provides novel insights into diverse watermarking paradigms. Our code is available at \href{https://github.com/redwyd/SymMark}{https://github.com/redwyd/SymMark}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From Trade-off to Synergy: A Versatile Symbiotic Watermarking Framework for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of watermarking Large Language Model (LLM) generated text, specifically the inherent trade-offs between robustness, text quality, and security present in existing logits-based and sampling-based watermarking methods.  The authors propose a novel symbiotic watermarking framework, *SymMark*, that integrates both logits-based and sampling-based approaches to achieve synergy. The framework offers three strategies: Serial, Parallel, and Hybrid.  The Hybrid strategy adaptively embeds watermarks using token entropy and semantic entropy to optimize the balance between detectability, robustness, text quality, and security. They validate the framework through comprehensive experiments on various datasets and models, demonstrating superior performance compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining logits-based and sampling-based watermarking is novel and addresses a significant limitation in the current literature. The adaptive Hybrid approach, guided by token and semantic entropy, is a key innovation for balancing the trade-offs. While individual components like entropy-based selection have been explored in other contexts, the systematic integration of these methods into a symbiotic framework represents a genuine contribution.
*   **Significance:**  Watermarking LLM outputs is crucial for addressing issues like AI-generated content misuse, intellectual property protection, and disinformation. A framework that effectively balances robustness, text quality, and security has substantial practical importance. The paper's findings offer valuable insights into designing more effective watermarking strategies for LLMs. The comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and models strengthens the credibility of their results. The release of the code also enhances the paper's impact by facilitating further research and adoption.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies and articulates a significant problem.
    *   The proposed *SymMark* framework provides a well-defined solution with several strategies.
    *   The adaptive Hybrid approach is a sophisticated and promising technique.
    *   The extensive experimental evaluation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of *SymMark*.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly structured.
    *   Releasing the code increases reproducibility and adoption.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper touches upon the security aspect, more in-depth analysis against specific watermark stealing attacks (beyond the one included) would be valuable. The resilience of the hybrid approach in the face of a determined adversary reverse-engineering the entropy-based decision function could be more critically examined.
    *   The reliance on token and semantic entropy, while effective, might have limitations in specific scenarios or languages. A discussion of potential edge cases or alternative entropy measures would add depth.
    *   The implementation details section could be more detailed regarding the rationale behind choosing specific thresholds and hyperparameters. While appendix G provides some analysis, a more explicit justification in the main text would improve clarity.
    *   The paper could benefit from a clearer discussion of the computational overhead associated with entropy calculations, especially for very large models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research on LLM watermarking. The symbiotic approach could become a standard paradigm for addressing the trade-offs in watermark design. The entropy-based adaptation technique could be adopted and extended in various ways. The paper's findings could also inform the development of more robust and secure watermarking methods.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM watermarking.  The symbiotic approach, particularly the adaptive Hybrid strategy, offers a compelling solution to the trade-offs inherent in existing methods. The rigorous experimental evaluation and clear presentation strengthen the paper's credibility. While there are some weaknesses, particularly regarding security analysis and limitations of entropy-based decisions, the overall impact and potential influence of the paper warrant a high score.

### Reinforced Interactive Continual Learning via Real-time Noisy Human Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09925v1)
- **Authors**: Yutao Yang, Jie Zhou, Junsong Li, Qianjun Pan, Bihao Zhan, Qin Chen, Xipeng Qiu, Liang He
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces an interactive continual learning paradigm where AI models dynamically learn new skills from real-time human feedback while retaining prior knowledge. This paradigm distinctively addresses two major limitations of traditional continual learning: (1) dynamic model updates using streaming, real-time human-annotated data, rather than static datasets with fixed labels, and (2) the assumption of clean labels, by explicitly handling the noisy feedback common in real-world interactions. To tackle these problems, we propose RiCL, a Reinforced interactive Continual Learning framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to learn new skills effectively from dynamic feedback. RiCL incorporates three key components: a temporal consistency-aware purifier to automatically discern clean from noisy samples in data streams; an interaction-aware direct preference optimization strategy to align model behavior with human intent by reconciling AI-generated and human-provided feedback; and a noise-resistant contrastive learning module that captures robust representations by exploiting inherent data relationships, thus avoiding reliance on potentially unreliable labels. Extensive experiments on two benchmark datasets (FewRel and TACRED), contaminated with realistic noise patterns, demonstrate that our RiCL approach substantially outperforms existing combinations of state-of-the-art online continual learning and noisy-label learning methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RiCL, a Reinforced Interactive Continual Learning framework for training AI models using real-time, noisy human feedback.  RiCL addresses limitations of traditional continual learning by handling dynamic data streams with noisy labels.  It incorporates three main components: a temporal consistency-aware purifier (TCP) to distinguish clean from noisy samples, an interaction-aware direct preference optimization (IPO) strategy to align model behavior with human intent, and a noise-resistant contrastive learning (NCL) module to learn robust representations. Experiments on the FewRel and Tacred datasets demonstrate that RiCL outperforms existing combinations of online continual learning and noisy-label learning methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual components of RiCL (TCP, IPO, and NCL) have precedents in the literature, the *integration* of these components within a continual learning framework specifically designed for real-time, noisy human feedback is a significant contribution.  Prior work often treats continual learning and noisy labels as separate problems or relies on static datasets with pre-defined labels. The explicit focus on *interactive* continual learning with noisy human feedback is a relatively unexplored area. Existing online continual learning approaches typically assume clean labels, while noisy label methods usually operate on pre-prepared datasets. The authors successfully combine and adapt these techniques for a novel application.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its practical relevance to real-world AI systems.  Interactive AI applications inevitably involve dynamic data streams and noisy human input.  RiCL provides a more realistic and robust approach to continual learning in such scenarios. By effectively handling noise and incorporating human feedback, it has the potential to improve the performance and reliability of AI systems used in personalized assistance, autonomous systems, and other human-in-the-loop applications. However, the reliance on LLMs raises concerns regarding computational cost, and the simulated datasets do not capture the full complexity of human interaction.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined problem and motivation.
    *   Well-structured framework with modular components.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation against strong baselines.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in both mitigating catastrophic forgetting and handling label noise.
    *   Ablation studies that highlight the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulated Datasets:** The experiments are conducted on simulated datasets with synthetic noise, which may not fully reflect the complexity and biases of real human feedback. Human feedback can be inconsistent, subjective, and context-dependent, aspects not fully captured by the experimental setup. This represents a significant limitation in assessing real-world performance.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework is grounded in LLMs, which raises concerns about computational costs and accessibility. While LLMs are powerful, their resource requirements can be a barrier to adoption in some applications.
    *   **Symmetric Noise Assumption:** The noise model assumes symmetric label noise. Real-world noise is often asymmetric, with certain types of errors being more likely than others.  The paper does not address the robustness of RiCL to asymmetric noise patterns.
    *   **Limited interaction types:** The framework only supports single-turn feedback, which might not be sufficient for complex tasks.
    *   **Lack of real-world evaluation:** The absence of real-world deployment results is a major drawback. Although the performance in simulated environments provides valuable information, its utility is limited without practical evidence.

*   **Potential Impact:** RiCL's joint feedback-focused design offers an essential strategy for solving for issues within interactive continual learning, and serves as a strong starting point to solve label noise and forgetting. It narrows the gap between offline continual learning and noisy label learning paradigms. The findings can inform future research directions in AI, particularly in the areas of human-computer interaction and adaptive learning systems. The design choices may influence the development of more robust and reliable AI systems that can learn effectively from noisy and dynamic data.

* **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the practical challenges of continual learning with noisy human feedback. The proposed RiCL framework is well-designed, thoroughly evaluated, and shows promising results on benchmark datasets. However, the use of simulated data, reliance on LLMs, and assumption of symmetric noise limit the generalizability of the findings and point to avenues for future research.
