The idea of modeling and incorporating "hidden thoughts" during pretraining is a step beyond simply fine-tuning or relying on curated, task-specific datasets. While the use of synthetic data for training LLMs is not entirely new, the specific focus on simulating human-like reasoning processes is relatively novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have the potential to impact how reasoning capabilities are instilled in LLMs. The observed improvements across different domains and problem difficulties suggest that the proposed method might be a valuable addition to current techniques. The potential for cross-domain transfer, if robust, is particularly significant, as it could alleviate the need for extensive task-specific data.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current reasoning training methods (SFT, RL) and provides a compelling motivation for exploring CPT with synthetic reasoning data.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The approach is well-defined and clearly explained. The synthetic data generation process and the Reasoning CPT procedure are easy to follow.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments are relatively comprehensive, covering different domains, difficulty levels, and comparisons with baseline methods.  The MMLU benchmark is a suitable choice for evaluating general reasoning ability.
    *   **Interesting Findings:**  The results related to cross-domain transfer and adaptive reasoning length are intriguing and deserve further investigation.
    *   **Code/Data Sharing**: If the authors released the code for hidden thought generation and CPT setup, this would further enhance the reproducibility and impact of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Data Quality:** The quality and realism of the generated "hidden thoughts" are a critical factor. The paper acknowledges using Gemma2-9B-it for this purpose, but a more in-depth analysis of the synthetic data itself would be beneficial. A qualitative analysis, showing examples of good and bad generated "thoughts", would strengthen the paper. It's unclear if the generated thoughts are truly representative of human reasoning.
    *   **Limited Model Size:** While the choice of Gemma2-9B is practical from a computational standpoint, it's unclear how well the findings will scale to larger and more powerful LLMs. Experiments with models exceeding 70B+ parameters could demonstrate the true potential of the method.
    *   **Benchmark Dependence:**  While MMLU is a valuable benchmark, it primarily assesses factual knowledge and multiple-choice question-answering. Other benchmarks with more complex reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical inference) would provide a more robust evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Study**: While standard CPT is used as a baseline for comparison, the authors should consider ablate this experimental design. For example, how does the performance of a model continually pretrained on text sequences of only generated "hidden thoughts" compare to Reasoning CPT? This may offer more compelling evidence on the significance of hidden thoughts in LLM Reasoning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in several directions:
    *   Encouraging further exploration of synthetic data generation techniques for improving LLM reasoning.
    *   Inspiring new methods for modeling and incorporating internal reasoning processes into LLM training.
    *   Motivating the development of more robust and diverse benchmarks for evaluating LLM reasoning abilities.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a promising and novel approach to LLM reasoning using synthetic data. However, the lack of a rigorous analysis of the synthetic data quality, limited model size in the experiments, and dependence on MMLU for evaluation temper its significance. A 7/10 is merited, reflecting the good conceptual novelty and potential impact.

**Score: 7/10**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The CoT Encyclopedia: Analyzing, Predicting, and Controlling how a Reasoning Model will Think
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10185v1)
- **Authors**: Seongyun Lee, Seungone Kim, Minju Seo, Yongrae Jo, Dongyoung Go, Hyeonbin Hwang, Jinho Park, Xiang Yue, Sean Welleck, Graham Neubig, Moontae Lee, Minjoon Seo
- **Abstract**: Long chain-of-thought (CoT) is an essential ingredient in effective usage of modern large language models, but our understanding of the reasoning strategies underlying these capabilities remains limited. While some prior works have attempted to categorize CoTs using predefined strategy types, such approaches are constrained by human intuition and fail to capture the full diversity of model behaviors. In this work, we introduce the CoT Encyclopedia, a bottom-up framework for analyzing and steering model reasoning. Our method automatically extracts diverse reasoning criteria from model-generated CoTs, embeds them into a semantic space, clusters them into representative categories, and derives contrastive rubrics to interpret reasoning behavior. Human evaluations show that this framework produces more interpretable and comprehensive analyses than existing methods. Moreover, we demonstrate that this understanding enables performance gains: we can predict which strategy a model is likely to use and guide it toward more effective alternatives. Finally, we provide practical insights, such as that training data format (e.g., free-form vs. multiple-choice) has a far greater impact on reasoning behavior than data domain, underscoring the importance of format-aware model design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The COT ENCYCLOPEDIA: Analyzing, Predicting, and Controlling how a Reasoning Model will Think":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces the "COT ENCYCLOPEDIA," a novel, bottom-up framework for analyzing and steering reasoning strategies in large language models (LLMs) using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting.  Instead of relying on predefined reasoning strategy categories, the method automatically extracts diverse reasoning criteria from model-generated CoTs, embeds them into a semantic space, clusters them to identify representative categories, and derives contrastive rubrics for interpretability.  The authors demonstrate that this framework leads to more comprehensive analyses than existing methods. Moreover, this understanding enables improved model performance by predicting and guiding model strategy selection towards more effective alternatives.  The paper also presents insights into how training data format (e.g., free-form vs. multiple-choice) significantly impacts reasoning behavior, more so than data domain.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution, the COT ENCYCLOPEDIA framework, represents a significant advancement in understanding and controlling LLM reasoning. The bottom-up approach to identifying reasoning strategies is a departure from prior top-down methods, offering a more data-driven and potentially less biased perspective on how models actually reason. The ability to derive contrastive rubrics and use them for prediction and control is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in the field: the lack of deep understanding of LLM reasoning strategies despite CoT prompting's success. The COT ENCYCLOPEDIA provides a practical tool for researchers to systematically analyze and improve model reasoning capabilities. The findings on the impact of training data format, especially its greater influence than data domain, offer important guidelines for model training and design. The demonstrated ability to improve model performance through strategy control is also significant, suggesting potential for optimization in various applications. It also touches on an interesting aspect in that models tend to use similar strategies for similar problems, this helps set the framework in identifying potentially optimal solutions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Data-Driven Approach:**  The bottom-up strategy analysis overcomes limitations of predefined criteria and better reflects models' inherent behaviors.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The generated rubrics are interpretable and offer concrete guidance for model improvement.
    *   **Performance Gains:** Successfully demonstrates the feasibility of controlling reasoning strategies to improve accuracy across several benchmarks.
    *   **Novel Findings:** The findings on the dominant role of data format in shaping reasoning strategies are significant and practically relevant.
    *   **Comprehensive framework:** The design of the tool as an end-to-end framework can provide the field with a useful starting point for analyzing model behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLM Annotations:** The framework relies on LLMs (GPT-4o) for both generating and evaluating reasoning strategies. This could introduce bias or limitations based on the LLM's own reasoning patterns and biases. Further validation with more human evaluations would strengthens the claim.
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** While the paper presents results on several benchmarks, the scope could be expanded to include a wider range of tasks (e.g., scientific reasoning, code generation, multi-modal tasks) and model architectures to confirm the generality of the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The COT ENCYCLOPEDIA is likely to influence future research in LLM reasoning and control. It provides a valuable framework for analyzing model behavior, identifying effective reasoning strategies, and improving model performance. The insights into the impact of training data format will also inform model design and training strategies. The overall effect on the responsible deployment of LLMs is a positive one.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper represents a significant and novel contribution to the field, providing a practical and data-driven framework for understanding and controlling LLM reasoning. The demonstrated performance gains and the insights into training data format further enhance its value. However, the reliance on LLM annotations and the limited scope of evaluation slightly reduce the score.

### VQ-Logits: Compressing the Output Bottleneck of Large Language Models via Vector Quantized Logits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10202v1)
- **Authors**: Jintian Shao, Hongyi Huang, Jiayi Wu, YiMing Cheng, ZhiYu Wu, You Shan, MingKai Zheng
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success but face significant computational and memory challenges, particularly due to their extensive output vocabularies. The final linear projection layer, mapping hidden states to vocabulary-sized logits, often constitutes a substantial portion of the model's parameters and computational cost during inference. Existing methods like adaptive softmax or hierarchical softmax introduce structural complexities. In this paper, we propose VQ-Logits, a novel approach that leverages Vector Quantization (VQ) to drastically reduce the parameter count and computational load of the LLM output layer. VQ-Logits replaces the large V * dmodel output embedding matrix with a small, shared codebook of K embedding vectors (K << V ). Each token in the vocabulary is mapped to one of these K codebook vectors. The LLM predicts logits over this compact codebook, which are then efficiently "scattered" to the full vocabulary space using the learned or preassigned mapping. We demonstrate through extensive experiments on standard language modeling benchmarks (e.g., WikiText-103, C4) that VQ-Logits can achieve up to 99% parameter reduction in the output layer and 6x speedup in logit computation, with only a marginal 4% increase in perplexity compared to full softmax baselines. We further provide detailed ablation studies on codebook size, initialization, and learning strategies, showcasing the robustness and effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VQ-Logits: Compressing the Output Bottleneck of Large Language Models via Vector Quantized Logits":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VQ-Logits, a novel method to compress the output layer of large language models (LLMs) using vector quantization.  The key idea is to replace the large output projection matrix (mapping hidden states to vocabulary logits) with a much smaller, learnable codebook.  Each token in the vocabulary is then mapped to one of these codebook vectors. The LLM predicts logits over this compact codebook, which are then "scattered" to the full vocabulary space based on the pre-defined mapping. This significantly reduces the number of parameters and the computational cost of the output layer.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of VQ-Logits on standard language modeling benchmarks like WikiText-103, Penn Treebank, and a subset of C4, showing substantial parameter reduction and speedup in logit computation with minimal performance degradation (measured by perplexity). They explore different codebook initialization and learning strategies, highlighting the robustness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of applying vector quantization directly to the *output embeddings* of an LLM is relatively novel. While vector quantization is a well-established technique, its application in this specific context, to directly compress the output layer *without* resorting to more complex factorization techniques or tree-based structures, distinguishes it. It directly addresses the ever-growing vocabulary size problem, presenting a straightforward solution. Prior work has used quantization for model compression generally (weights, activations), but targeting the computationally expensive output projection layer directly is innovative. The "scatter" operation to reconstruct the full vocabulary space is also a valuable contribution.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to make LLMs more accessible and deployable on resource-constrained devices. The output layer often represents a substantial portion of a model's parameters and computational cost, especially with large vocabularies. The proposed VQ-Logits method directly addresses this bottleneck, making it a practical solution for model compression and acceleration. The reduction in parameters and the speedup in logit computation are compelling results.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Simplicity:** The VQ-Logits method is conceptually simple and relatively easy to implement compared to some other output layer compression techniques.
    * **Effectiveness:**  The experimental results demonstrate a compelling trade-off between compression/speed and perplexity.  The authors show significant parameter reduction and speedup with only a marginal increase in perplexity.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes thorough experiments on multiple datasets, ablation studies on different initialization and learning strategies, and detailed analysis of speed and memory usage.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the VQ-Logits method and its implementation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Mapping Dependency:** The performance of VQ-Logits heavily relies on a good vocabulary-to-codebook mapping M. The current implementation relies on pre-computing M using k-means, which might not always be optimal, and can introduce some sensitivity to the quality of the initial output embeddings. An end-to-end differentiable approach for learning M jointly with the codebook would be more ideal (although the authors acknowledge this as future work).
    * **Hard Assignments:**  The hard assignments of vocabulary items to codebook vectors could lead to information loss and limit the model's expressive power. Soft assignments might improve performance, at the cost of added complexity.
    * **Generalizability:** While the paper presents results on several datasets, further evaluation on a wider range of LLMs and downstream tasks would strengthen the claims of generalizability.  Specifically, evaluating the impact on transfer learning performance and zero/few-shot learning would be valuable.
    * **Lack of comparison to state-of-the-art low-rank factorization techniques:**  While low-rank factorization is tested as a baseline, a more careful tuning and comparison with advanced methods like *sparse* low-rank factorization would be helpful.

* **Potential Impact:**  The VQ-Logits method has the potential to be widely adopted in the field of LLMs, especially for deployment on edge devices and in resource-constrained environments. It is a practical and effective technique for compressing the output layer, making it easier to work with large vocabulary sizes. The simplicity of the method and its competitive performance characteristics are likely to encourage its use in real-world applications.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and effective method (VQ-Logits) for compressing the output layer of LLMs. Its simplicity and practical benefits (significant parameter reduction and speedup with minimal performance loss) make it a valuable contribution to the field. The experimental results are strong and well-analyzed. The primary drawbacks are the dependence on a pre-computed mapping, and potential improvements available from learning a more flexible, end-to-end mapping and using softer assignments. While its core innovation is well-executed, there are readily apparent extensions and future research directions. A more rigorous comparison against advanced state-of-the-art methods would strengthen the paper further. The overall impact, given the growing importance of LLM deployment, is significant.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do LLMs Memorize Recommendation Datasets? A Preliminary Study on MovieLens-1M
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10212v1)
- **Authors**: Dario Di Palma, Felice Antonio Merra, Maurizio Sfilio, Vito Walter Anelli, Fedelucio Narducci, Tommaso Di Noia
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become increasingly central to recommendation scenarios due to their remarkable natural language understanding and generation capabilities. Although significant research has explored the use of LLMs for various recommendation tasks, little effort has been dedicated to verifying whether they have memorized public recommendation dataset as part of their training data. This is undesirable because memorization reduces the generalizability of research findings, as benchmarking on memorized datasets does not guarantee generalization to unseen datasets. Furthermore, memorization can amplify biases, for example, some popular items may be recommended more frequently than others. In this work, we investigate whether LLMs have memorized public recommendation datasets. Specifically, we examine two model families (GPT and Llama) across multiple sizes, focusing on one of the most widely used dataset in recommender systems: MovieLens-1M. First, we define dataset memorization as the extent to which item attributes, user profiles, and user-item interactions can be retrieved by prompting the LLMs. Second, we analyze the impact of memorization on recommendation performance. Lastly, we examine whether memorization varies across model families and model sizes. Our results reveal that all models exhibit some degree of memorization of MovieLens-1M, and that recommendation performance is related to the extent of memorization. We have made all the code publicly available at: https://github.com/sisinflab/LLM-MemoryInspector
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Do LLMs Memorize Recommendation Datasets? A Preliminary Study on MovieLens-1M":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) memorize public recommendation datasets, focusing specifically on the MovieLens-1M dataset. The authors examine different LLM families (GPT and Llama) across various model sizes. They define memorization in the context of recommendation datasets as the ability of an LLM to retrieve item attributes, user profiles, and user-item interactions when prompted.  The study analyzes the extent of memorization, its impact on recommendation performance, and how memorization varies across model families and sizes. The results indicate that LLMs do memorize MovieLens-1M to varying degrees, and this memorization is correlated with recommendation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a crucial but previously under-explored question: do LLMs simply "memorize" recommendation datasets instead of genuinely learning patterns?  While memorization in LLMs has been studied in other domains (e.g., text generation, code), its implications for *recommendation* are novel. Identifying the potential pitfalls of LLMs relying on memorized data (non-generalizable results, bias amplification, unfair comparisons) is also a valuable contribution. The adaptation of memorization definitions from general LLMs to the specific context of recommendation is commendable.

*   **Significance:** This work has significant implications for the evaluation of LLMs as recommenders.  If LLMs memorize benchmark datasets, standard evaluation metrics become unreliable, potentially leading to inflated performance claims. The observed popularity bias in memorization is also significant. The paper highlights a potential flaw in current evaluation protocols that needs to be addressed to ensure the development of truly generalizable and fair recommendation systems. This is especially important as LLMs increasingly find their way into real-world recommender systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines memorization within the context of recommendation.
    *   The use of prompt-based methods to probe LLMs for memorized information is a reasonable approach.
    *   The study examines a range of LLM families and sizes, providing a more comprehensive understanding of the issue.
    *   The analysis of popularity bias within memorized data is insightful.
    *   The authors clearly articulate the implications of their findings for evaluation practices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study focuses solely on MovieLens-1M. While this is a widely used dataset, generalizing the findings to other datasets requires further investigation.
    *   The prompt engineering is relatively simple (few-shot examples).  More sophisticated prompt engineering techniques might reveal even higher levels of memorization.
    *   The evaluation of recommendation performance is limited. It doesn't explicitly disentangle the contribution of memorization from genuine learning. It would be beneficial to incorporate methods that can effectively filter out the information presented in the dataset.
    *   The mitigation strategies for memorization and techniques to avoid it are not discussed, although mentioned as a future direction.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper's focus on dataset memorization by LLMs in the recommendation domain represents a valuable contribution that has not been thoroughly studied before. Although limited in scope (single dataset and simple probing), its implications for evaluation and the potential for bias are considerable. The paper acts as a crucial wake-up call to the community, urging a re-evaluation of evaluation practices. While there are some weaknesses, the novelty and significance of the problem being addressed warrant a relatively high score. The paper would further improve if the authors attempted more sophisticated prompt engineering techniques and explored additional datasets.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Informed Forecasting: Leveraging Auxiliary Knowledge to Boost LLM Performance on Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10213v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammadmahdi Ghasemloo, Alireza Moradi
- **Abstract**: With the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs), there is a growing need to establish best practices for leveraging their capabilities beyond traditional natural language tasks. In this paper, a novel cross-domain knowledge transfer framework is proposed to enhance the performance of LLMs in time series forecasting -- a task of increasing relevance in fields such as energy systems, finance, and healthcare. The approach systematically infuses LLMs with structured temporal information to improve their forecasting accuracy. This study evaluates the proposed method on a real-world time series dataset and compares it to a naive baseline where the LLM receives no auxiliary information. Results show that knowledge-informed forecasting significantly outperforms the uninformed baseline in terms of predictive accuracy and generalization. These findings highlight the potential of knowledge transfer strategies to bridge the gap between LLMs and domain-specific forecasting tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in time series forecasting by systematically incorporating structured temporal information (covariates) into the LLM's input. The authors focus on time-dependent covariates available within the dataset and propose an optimized prompting structure to prevent information leakage. They evaluate different covariate sets and their impact on forecasting accuracy using OpenAI's GPT-40-mini model and weekly influenza case data. The results demonstrate that knowledge-informed forecasting, achieved through their proposed prompting strategy, significantly outperforms uninformed baselines.  The paper emphasizes designing prompt engineering strategies to structure covariates for maximum utility, preventing information leakage and systematically evaluating the covariate combinations to meaningfully enhance performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the structured approach to incorporating covariates into LLM prompts for time series forecasting and the systematic evaluation of different covariate combinations and prompt structures.  While other papers have explored LLMs for time series, the explicit focus on maximizing information gain from covariates, especially with consideration to time dependencies within datasets, using only provided data, makes this distinctive. The design and comparison of Coupled, Decoupled and Contextual prompt structures represent novel and valuable experimentation.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in demonstrating a practical method for bridging the gap between LLMs and domain-specific forecasting tasks. Time series forecasting is relevant across many domains (energy, finance, healthcare), and a robust method for leveraging auxiliary knowledge can significantly improve LLM-based forecasts in these domains. This can lead to increased accuracy in tasks such as predicting energy demand, financial trends, and disease outbreaks, providing better decision-making tools. The results showed significant predictive accuracy improvement from the framework

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the challenges of applying LLMs to time series forecasting.
    *   The methodology is well-defined and the prompt engineering techniques are practical and replicable.
    *   The use of a real-world dataset (influenza cases) and a known model (GPT-40-mini) provides a realistic evaluation.
    *   The comparative analysis of different covariate sets and prompt structures is thorough and insightful.
    *   Focusing on providing all necessary data to the model, avoids issues of data leakage from external factors.
    * The writing is clear and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on a proprietary LLM (GPT-40-mini) limits reproducibility. While OpenAI models are commonly used, using open-source alternatives would increase accessibility and verifiability. The analysis did not explore the computational efficiency overhead from the method.
    *   The study focuses on a single dataset (influenza cases).  The findings' generalizability to other time series datasets with different characteristics (e.g., economic data, sensor data) needs further investigation.  The dataset is publicly available, enabling others to verify their results.
    *   While the paper discusses information leakage, more rigorous testing and formal proofs relating to leakage prevention could strengthen the paper.
    * It could be more explicit on how this approach could be integrated into existing forecasting workflows.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more effective LLM-based forecasting tools and encourage further research into prompt engineering for incorporating domain-specific knowledge. It suggests a path to improve the accuracy of LLMs in real-world, decision-making tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** I am assigning a score of 7. The paper has a notable novel framework to address the limitations of LLMs in time series forecasting by structuring covariate incorporation and avoiding leakage concerns. The framework has clear and well-articulated strengths and results showing predictive improvements.
    However, it is brought down by the proprietary LLM (decreasing replicability), the focus on a single dataset (reducing generalizability), and the lack of comparison with more complex time series models.  Therefore, I cannot score it much higher.

### RAIDEN-R1: Improving Role-awareness of LLMs via GRPO with Verifiable Reward
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10218v1)
- **Authors**: Zongsheng Wang, Kaili Sun, Bowen Wu, Qun Yu, Ying Li, Baoxun Wang
- **Abstract**: Role-playing conversational agents (RPCAs) face persistent challenges in maintaining role consistency. To address this, we propose RAIDEN-R1, a novel reinforcement learning framework that integrates Verifiable Role-Awareness Reward (VRAR). The method introduces both singular and multi-term mining strategies to generate quantifiable rewards by assessing role-specific keys. Additionally, we construct a high-quality, role-aware Chain-of-Thought dataset through multi-LLM collaboration, and implement experiments to enhance reasoning coherence. Experiments on the RAIDEN benchmark demonstrate RAIDEN-R1's superiority: our 14B-GRPO model achieves 88.04% and 88.65% accuracy on Script-Based Knowledge and Conversation Memory metrics, respectively, outperforming baseline models while maintaining robustness. Case analyses further reveal the model's enhanced ability to resolve conflicting contextual cues and sustain first-person narrative consistency. This work bridges the non-quantifiability gap in RPCA training and provides insights into role-aware reasoning patterns, advancing the development of RPCAs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the RAIDER-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RAIDEN-R1: Improving Role-awareness of LLMs via GRPO with Verifiable Reward" introduces a novel reinforcement learning framework, RAIDEN-R1, designed to enhance the role consistency of role-playing conversational agents (RPCAs). A key contribution is the "Verifiable Role-Awareness Reward" (VRAR) mechanism, which employs both single and multi-term mining strategies to generate quantifiable rewards based on role-specific key aspects.  The authors also construct a high-quality, role-aware Chain-of-Thought (CoT) dataset through multi-LLM collaboration and implement GRPO.  Experiments demonstrate that the 14B-GRPO model achieves improved performance on Script-Based Knowledge and Conversation Memory metrics, maintaining robustness compared to baseline models. The paper also provides insights into role-aware reasoning patterns in RPCAs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has some novel components, but it is incremental in nature. The combination of GRPO with a custom reward function and a specific CoT dataset fine-tuned for role-playing represents a practical engineering contribution. While individually these components are not entirely new, the specific *combination* and application to the RPCA domain, along with the verifiable reward function, constitute a contribution. The most novel aspect is perhaps the "Verifiable Role-Awareness Reward" (VRAR) itself. However, it's still a rule-based reward, not something learned.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in addressing a critical challenge in RPCAs: maintaining role consistency. The VRAR mechanism provides a means to quantify and optimize role-awareness, which can be useful for improving the reliability and believability of RPCAs. The construction of a high-quality, role-aware CoT dataset is also a valuable contribution, as it can be used to train more effective RPCAs. The case studies provide valuable insights into how role-aware reasoning can be enhanced through GRPO. However, the experiments are primarily focused on demonstrating the improvement compared to a baseline and limited in scale.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Quantifiable reward function (VRAR) addresses a key limitation in RPCA training.
    *   Creation of a high-quality CoT dataset.
    *   Demonstrated improvements over baseline models through GRPO.
    *   Insightful case studies illustrating enhanced role-aware reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. VRAR is a rule-based system, thus not learning any reward.
    *   Limited evaluation: The experiments could benefit from a more comprehensive evaluation, potentially against a wider range of state-of-the-art RPCA models and with larger scale datasets. Also, the models used were not the best ones (e.g., Qwen2.5 rather than GPT-4 or Gemini).
    *   The gains, while present, are not always massive.
    *   The cold-start SFT experiments did not lead to improved RPCA metrics and actually performed worse.
    *   The code and models not being open-sourced hinders reproducibility and further research.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the development of more robust and believable RPCAs. The VRAR mechanism and CoT dataset can be used as building blocks for other researchers in the field. The insights from the case studies can help inform the design of future RPCA models and training strategies. However, the lack of open-sourcing somewhat reduces potential for broader adoption and impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above assessment, the paper makes a worthwhile, but incremental contribution to the field of RPCA development. While it has some novel elements and practical value, its incremental nature, limited evaluation, and experimental results, and the lack of open-sourcing prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Score**: 6/10

### ComplexFormer: Disruptively Advancing Transformer Inference Ability via Head-Specific Complex Vector Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10222v1)
- **Authors**: Jintian Shao, Hongyi Huang, Jiayi Wu, Beiwen Zhang, ZhiYu Wu, You Shan, MingKai Zheng
- **Abstract**: Transformer models rely on self-attention to capture token dependencies but face challenges in effectively integrating positional information while allowing multi-head attention (MHA) flexibility. Prior methods often model semantic and positional differences disparately or apply uniform positional adjustments across heads, potentially limiting representational capacity. This paper introduces ComplexFormer, featuring Complex Multi-Head Attention-CMHA. CMHA empowers each head to independently model semantic and positional differences unified within the complex plane, representing interactions as rotations and scaling. ComplexFormer incorporates two key improvements: (1) a per-head Euler transformation, converting real-valued query/key projections into polar-form complex vectors for head-specific complex subspace operation; and (2) a per-head adaptive differential rotation mechanism, exp[i(Adapt(ASmn,i) + Delta(Pmn),i)], allowing each head to learn distinct strategies for integrating semantic angle differences (ASmn,i) with relative positional encodings (Delta(Pmn),i). Extensive experiments on language modeling, text generation, code generation, and mathematical reasoning show ComplexFormer achieves superior performance, significantly lower generation perplexity , and improved long-context coherence compared to strong baselines like RoPE-Transformers. ComplexFormer demonstrates strong parameter efficiency, offering a more expressive, adaptable attention mechanism.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ComplexFormer paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ComplexFormer, a novel transformer architecture modification that enhances the multi-head attention mechanism (MHA) by integrating complex-valued computations. The core idea is to allow each attention head to independently model semantic and positional information within the complex plane, unifying interactions as rotations and scaling. This is achieved through two key components: a per-head Euler transformation to convert real-valued query/key projections into polar-form complex vectors, and a per-head adaptive differential rotation mechanism, allowing each head to learn distinct strategies for integrating semantic angle differences with relative positional encodings. The ComplexFormer is evaluated on various tasks including language modeling, code generation, and mathematical reasoning, demonstrating superior performance compared to strong baselines, especially in generation perplexity and long-context coherence, with competitive parameter efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to integrate positional information into the attention mechanism by using complex numbers and per-head adaptive rotations. This departs from traditional methods that often handle semantic and positional information separately or apply uniform adjustments across heads. The specific mechanisms—per-head Euler transformation and adaptive differential rotation—are reasonably novel in the context of transformer attention mechanisms.  While complex-valued networks and EulerFormer (applied to recommendations) exist, the adaptation to MHA for language models and the head-specific aspect are critical distinctions. The idea of complex attention isn't brand new, but its *application* and particularly the *adaptive per-head* aspect are valuable contributions.

**Significance:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements across various challenging tasks. The improved generation perplexity and long-context coherence are particularly noteworthy. The reported parameter efficiency is also important, suggesting that the ComplexFormer can achieve better performance with a similar or smaller parameter count compared to baselines like RoPE-Transformers. The ablation studies highlighting the importance of head-specific adaptivity further strengthens the significance of the proposed approach.  A significant result is the boost in code and math generation capabilities which usually benefits from enhanced positional embeddings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing transformer architectures in effectively integrating positional information while maintaining the flexibility of MHA.
*   **Novel Approach:** The proposed ComplexFormer architecture offers a unique solution to the identified problem by leveraging complex-valued computations and per-head adaptivity.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across multiple diverse tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
*   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the different components of the ComplexFormer architecture.
*   **Parameter Efficiency:**  The demonstrated parameter efficiency is a significant advantage in the era of large language models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Overhead:** While the paper acknowledges the computational overhead associated with complex number operations, it doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the actual runtime performance or energy consumption. More details regarding the absolute runtimes compared to RoPE transformers, including training and inference costs, would be welcome.
*   **Interpretability:** The paper mentions the challenges in interpreting the learned head specializations. Providing some preliminary analysis or visualization of the learned adaptation parameters could further enhance the understanding of the proposed approach.
*   **Extrapolation Limitations:** Although the paper improved code generation capabilities, a specific long-context assessment involving context lengths >4096 wasn't conducted. It may further help assessing the long context and extrapolation capabilities.

**Potential Impact:**

ComplexFormer can be considered a strong improvement. Its success across several applications, especially code-gen and reasoning, would suggest applicability. Its strength lies in better utilizing the multi-head self attention's ability to capture context information while improving its flexibility.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a well-motivated, novel, and empirically validated approach to enhance transformer attention mechanisms. While there are some limitations, such as the computational overhead and interpretability challenges, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. The achieved performance improvements, especially in generation perplexity and long-context coherence, combined with parameter efficiency, make this a valuable contribution to the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Score**: 7/10

### Comparing LLM Text Annotation Skills: A Study on Human Rights Violations in Social Media Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10260v1)
- **Authors**: Poli Apollinaire Nemkova, Solomon Ubani, Mark V. Albert
- **Abstract**: In the era of increasingly sophisticated natural language processing (NLP) systems, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable potential for diverse applications, including tasks requiring nuanced textual understanding and contextual reasoning. This study investigates the capabilities of multiple state-of-the-art LLMs - GPT-3.5, GPT-4, LLAMA3, Mistral 7B, and Claude-2 - for zero-shot and few-shot annotation of a complex textual dataset comprising social media posts in Russian and Ukrainian. Specifically, the focus is on the binary classification task of identifying references to human rights violations within the dataset. To evaluate the effectiveness of these models, their annotations are compared against a gold standard set of human double-annotated labels across 1000 samples. The analysis includes assessing annotation performance under different prompting conditions, with prompts provided in both English and Russian. Additionally, the study explores the unique patterns of errors and disagreements exhibited by each model, offering insights into their strengths, limitations, and cross-linguistic adaptability. By juxtaposing LLM outputs with human annotations, this research contributes to understanding the reliability and applicability of LLMs for sensitive, domain-specific tasks in multilingual contexts. It also sheds light on how language models handle inherently subjective and context-dependent judgments, a critical consideration for their deployment in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Comparing LLM Text Annotation Skills: A Study on Human Rights Violations in Social Media Data" investigates the ability of several large language models (LLMs), including GPT-3.5, GPT-4, Claude-2, LLaMA-3, and Mistral-7B, to identify references to human rights violations in social media posts written in Russian and Ukrainian. It compares the models' performance against a human-annotated gold standard, examining the impact of zero-shot and few-shot prompting, as well as the influence of prompt language (English vs. Russian). The study finds that GPT-4 generally performs best, aligning prompt language with the data language improves performance (especially for open-source models), and that all models struggle with ambiguous cases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Practical Relevance:** The research addresses a critical real-world application – human rights monitoring – where accurate and efficient text analysis is crucial.
    * **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The study systematically compares a diverse set of LLMs (closed-source and open-source) under different prompting conditions, providing valuable insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses.
    * **Multilingual Focus:**  The emphasis on Russian and Ukrainian data is a significant contribution, given the relative scarcity of research in these languages compared to English. The study correctly highlights that linguistic alignment is important.
    * **Prompt Engineering Insights:** The investigation of zero-shot and few-shot prompting strategies, along with the impact of prompt language, offers actionable guidance for practitioners.
    * **Error Analysis and Ablation Study:** The paper goes beyond simply reporting performance metrics by delving into the types of errors models make and performing an ablation study on agreed vs. disagreed human annotations. This is an important strength as it provides a nuanced understanding of model limitations.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Dataset Size:**  Although justified by computational costs, a sample size of 1000 posts is relatively small. A larger dataset would provide more statistically robust results and enhance the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Limited Prompt Engineering:** The study uses basic zero-shot and few-shot prompts. More sophisticated prompt engineering techniques (e.g., chain-of-thought prompting, instruction tuning, prompt ensembling) could potentially improve the performance of weaker models and further highlight the capabilities of stronger models. It is acknowledged in the limitations.
    * **Specificity of Findings:**  The findings are specific to the task of identifying human rights violations in Russian and Ukrainian social media.  While valuable, the generalizability to other domains and languages may be limited.
    * **Lack of Novelty in Architecture or Methodology:** The paper primarily applies existing LLMs and standard prompting techniques to a new dataset. It lacks a novel algorithmic contribution or a fundamentally new approach to using LLMs for text annotation.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The primary contribution is the systematic benchmarking of LLMs for a specific, important task (human rights monitoring) in a relatively under-explored multilingual setting. The paper provides practical guidance on prompt design and model selection for this task. While the methods used are standard, the application to this specific domain and the insights gained are valuable for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The ablation study into human error vs LLM error is helpful.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid empirical study with practically relevant findings. However, it lacks significant novelty in terms of methodology or algorithmic contributions. The limitations regarding dataset size and the scope of prompt engineering reduce the overall impact. The study fills a clear gap in the literature by addressing the performance of LLMs in a multilingual, high-stakes application domain.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Evolving Landscape of Generative Large Language Models and Traditional Natural Language Processing in Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10261v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Yang, Huitao Li, Matthew Yu Heng Wong, Yuhe Ke, Xin Li, Kunyu Yu, Jingchi Liao, Jonathan Chong Kai Liew, Sabarinath Vinod Nair, Jasmine Chiat Ling Ong, Irene Li, Douglas Teodoro, Chuan Hong, Daniel Shu Wei Ting, Nan Liu
- **Abstract**: Natural language processing (NLP) has been traditionally applied to medicine, and generative large language models (LLMs) have become prominent recently. However, the differences between them across different medical tasks remain underexplored. We analyzed 19,123 studies, finding that generative LLMs demonstrate advantages in open-ended tasks, while traditional NLP dominates in information extraction and analysis tasks. As these technologies advance, ethical use of them is essential to ensure their potential in medical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the evolving landscape of Generative Large Language Models (LLMs) and traditional Natural Language Processing (NLP) in medicine. It analyzes 19,123 studies to compare the task distributions and application contexts of both approaches. The findings suggest that generative LLMs demonstrate advantages in open-ended tasks (e.g., medical education, text summarization), while traditional NLP methods are dominant in information extraction and analysis tasks (e.g., electronic health records, named entity recognition). The paper concludes by discussing the ethical considerations and potential future directions for both technologies in medical applications, emphasizing responsible development and the continued importance of human expertise.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comparative analysis of LLMs and traditional NLP across a broad range of medical applications. While individual applications of LLMs in medicine are well-documented, a systematic comparison of the strengths and weaknesses of each approach within different task categories is relatively novel. The study employs topic modeling on a large corpus of research papers, providing a data-driven perspective on the distribution of research efforts.

*Significance:* The paper has some significance for researchers and practitioners in medical informatics and NLP. It provides a clearer understanding of where LLMs currently excel versus where traditional methods remain more effective. This insight can inform decisions about which techniques to apply to specific problems. The discussion of ethical considerations and responsible development is also timely and important.

*Strengths:*

*   *Systematic Approach:* The large-scale literature analysis using a rigorous methodology (PRISMA) and topic modeling provides a strong empirical foundation for the claims.
*   *Clear Findings:* The paper clearly articulates the complementary strengths of LLMs and traditional NLP methods, providing practical guidance for researchers and practitioners.
*   *Ethical Considerations:*  The inclusion of a discussion on ethical implications acknowledges the potential risks associated with these technologies and reinforces the need for responsible development.

*Weaknesses:*

*   *Limited Depth of Technical Analysis:* While the paper provides a broad overview, it lacks a deeper technical analysis of the specific algorithms or architectures used in the studies analyzed.  A more granular examination of performance metrics or model characteristics could have strengthened the conclusions.
*   *Somewhat Descriptive:* The study is largely descriptive, outlining the landscape without necessarily providing strong causal explanations for the observed patterns. Why *specifically* do traditional NLP methods perform better at information extraction? Further investigation into the underlying reasons could be valuable.
*   *Future Applicability:* The search was restricted to before March 1st, 2025, which is in the future at the time this paper was written, which could be seen as a flaw in the methodology of the paper.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper could influence research directions by highlighting areas where further investigation is needed. For example, more research is needed to improve the reasoning and explainability of LLMs in medical contexts. It might also encourage the development of hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of both LLMs and traditional NLP.

*Conclusion:*

Overall, the paper presents a valuable and timely overview of the evolving relationship between LLMs and traditional NLP in medicine.  While lacking a deep technical analysis, it provides useful insights based on a systematic review of a large body of literature. The findings can help guide future research and inform practical applications. Given these considerations, a score of 6 is warranted.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Questions to Clinical Recommendations: Large Language Models Driving Evidence-Based Clinical Decision Making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10282v1)
- **Authors**: Dubai Li, Nan Jiang, Kangping Huang, Ruiqi Tu, Shuyu Ouyang, Huayu Yu, Lin Qiao, Chen Yu, Tianshu Zhou, Danyang Tong, Qian Wang, Mengtao Li, Xiaofeng Zeng, Yu Tian, Xinping Tian, Jingsong Li
- **Abstract**: Clinical evidence, derived from rigorous research and data analysis, provides healthcare professionals with reliable scientific foundations for informed decision-making. Integrating clinical evidence into real-time practice is challenging due to the enormous workload, complex professional processes, and time constraints. This highlights the need for tools that automate evidence synthesis to support more efficient and accurate decision making in clinical settings. This study introduces Quicker, an evidence-based clinical decision support system powered by large language models (LLMs), designed to automate evidence synthesis and generate clinical recommendations modeled after standard clinical guideline development processes. Quicker implements a fully automated chain that covers all phases, from questions to clinical recommendations, and further enables customized decision-making through integrated tools and interactive user interfaces. To evaluate Quicker's capabilities, we developed the Q2CRBench-3 benchmark dataset, based on clinical guideline development records for three different diseases. Experimental results highlighted Quicker's strong performance, with fine-grained question decomposition tailored to user preferences, retrieval sensitivities comparable to human experts, and literature screening performance approaching comprehensive inclusion of relevant studies. In addition, Quicker-assisted evidence assessment effectively supported human reviewers, while Quicker's recommendations were more comprehensive and logically coherent than those of clinicians. In system-level testing, collaboration between a single reviewer and Quicker reduced the time required for recommendation development to 20-40 minutes. In general, our findings affirm the potential of Quicker to help physicians make quicker and more reliable evidence-based clinical decisions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, incorporating rigor and justification in the scoring:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Quicker," an evidence-based clinical decision support system powered by large language models (LLMs). Quicker aims to automate evidence synthesis and clinical recommendation generation, mirroring the clinical guideline development process.  The system's workflow comprises question decomposition, literature search, study selection, evidence assessment, and recommendation formulation. Interactive interfaces allow for human oversight and refinement. To evaluate Quicker, the authors developed the Q2CRBench-3 benchmark dataset based on clinical guideline development records across three diseases. Experimental results demonstrate strong performance in each phase, including fine-grained question decomposition, sensitive literature retrieval, effective study selection, and support for evidence assessment. System-level testing showed a reduction in recommendation development time with Quicker's assistance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive, end-to-end system for evidence-based clinical decision support. Unlike previous work that focuses on individual tasks or model-centric approaches, Quicker integrates multiple LLMs and tools into a workflow inspired by established clinical guideline development practices. This offers a potentially more reliable and trustworthy approach compared to systems built on unsupervised training or fragmented knowledge sources. The development of the Q2CRBench-3 dataset is also a significant contribution, providing a structured benchmark for evaluating similar systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **System-Level Approach:** The focus on a complete workflow, rather than individual tasks, addresses a key challenge in translating clinical evidence into practice.
    *   **Emphasis on Evidence-Based Medicine:** Grounding the system in EBM principles and incorporating human oversight adds credibility and promotes adoption.
    *   **Benchmark Dataset:** Q2CRBench-3 provides a valuable resource for evaluating and comparing future systems.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper demonstrates promising results across all phases of the workflow.
    *   **Transparency and User Control:** Interactive interfaces enable users to understand and refine the system's processes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the results are promising, the evaluation focuses on rheumatoid arthritis and specific datasets from established guidelines. Generalizability to other diseases and clinical settings needs further investigation.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 and DeepSeek:** The system's performance is dependent on these specific LLMs. While the comparison against other models mitigates this, its performance with future LLMs is unknown.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** Although it incorporates EBM, relying entirely on what LLMs can determine/scrape from pre-existing data still risks bias. The algorithm is as sound as its inputs, and therefore should also include a "freshness" feature to determine how current and accurate the available data is.

*   **Potential Influence on the Field:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of clinical decision support systems. It provides a blueprint for building trustworthy and reliable systems by grounding them in EBM principles and incorporating human oversight. The Q2CRBench-3 dataset will facilitate further research in this area. If widely adopted, Quicker-like systems could streamline the process of translating clinical evidence into practice, improve decision-making efficiency, and ultimately benefit patient care.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** Given that the model's success depends on the accuracy and integrity of data, further study should be performed for "freshness."

**Score: 7.5/10**

**Justification:** The paper makes a substantial contribution with its comprehensive system and the Q2CRBench-3 dataset. The strong experimental results and emphasis on EBM are also significant strengths. However, the somewhat limited evaluation scope and reliance on specific LLMs warrant a more cautious assessment. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's potential while acknowledging the need for further validation and generalization.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### StoryReasoning Dataset: Using Chain-of-Thought for Scene Understanding and Grounded Story Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10292v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel A. P. Oliveira, David Martins de Matos
- **Abstract**: Visual storytelling systems struggle to maintain character identity across frames and link actions to appropriate subjects, frequently leading to referential hallucinations. These issues can be addressed through grounding of characters, objects, and other entities on the visual elements. We propose StoryReasoning, a dataset containing 4,178 stories derived from 52,016 movie images, with both structured scene analyses and grounded stories. Each story maintains character and object consistency across frames while explicitly modeling multi-frame relationships through structured tabular representations. Our approach features cross-frame object re-identification using visual similarity and face recognition, chain-of-thought reasoning for explicit narrative modeling, and a grounding scheme that links textual elements to visual entities across multiple frames. We establish baseline performance by fine-tuning Qwen2.5-VL 7B, creating Qwen Storyteller, which performs end-to-end object detection, re-identification, and landmark detection while maintaining consistent object references throughout the story. Evaluation demonstrates a reduction from 4.06 to 3.56 (-12.3%) hallucinations on average per story when compared to a non-fine-tuned model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces StoryReasoning, a novel dataset designed to facilitate multi-frame visual storytelling systems that can maintain character and object consistency across a sequence of images. The dataset, derived from the GroundCap dataset, comprises 4,178 stories based on 52,016 movie images. It includes structured scene analyses (using chain-of-thought reasoning to model cross-frame consistency) and grounded narratives that link textual elements to specific visual entities. The authors also present Qwen Storyteller, a baseline model fine-tuned from Qwen2.5-VL, which performs end-to-end object detection, re-identification, and landmark detection while maintaining consistent object references throughout the generated stories. The evaluation shows a reduction in hallucination rate compared to the non-fine-tuned model.  An interactive visualization interface is also introduced for easier interpretation and access by users.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the construction of the StoryReasoning dataset, specifically designed for multi-frame visual storytelling with a focus on grounding and cross-frame consistency.  Existing datasets often lack the structured analyses and detailed grounding information provided here. The chain-of-thought framework applied to visual storytelling and the grounding scheme are also noteworthy contributions, though the techniques themselves are not entirely new, but their application to this specific task is original. The system combines several advances into a single end-to-end system.

*   **Significance:** Maintaining consistency and grounding are crucial challenges in visual storytelling. By providing a dataset specifically addressing these issues, the paper can potentially drive significant advancements in the field. The baseline model and the interactive visualization tool further contribute to the accessibility and impact of the work. The improvement in hallucination reduction is a tangible demonstration of the dataset's utility.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The StoryReasoning dataset is a valuable resource for researchers working on visual storytelling, especially given its focus on consistency and grounding.
    *   **Structured Approach:** The structured scene analysis with chain-of-thought reasoning provides explicit modeling of entities and relationships, which is beneficial for generating coherent narratives.
    *   **Grounded Narratives:** The XML-based grounding scheme allows for explicit linking between textual elements and visual entities, reducing ambiguity and hallucinations.
    *   **Baseline Model:** The Qwen Storyteller provides a strong baseline for future research and demonstrates the effectiveness of the dataset.
    *   **Interactive Interface:** The interface increases the usability of the dataset and model, allowing users to easily generate and interpret stories.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty of Techniques:** While the dataset and its components (grounding, analysis) are novel, the underlying techniques are built upon existing methods. The fine-tuning of an existing LLM (Qwen) is a relatively standard approach, although the results showcase the dataset's effectiveness.
    *   **Movie Domain Bias:** The dataset is derived from movies, which may introduce a bias towards cinematic composition and narrative structures that may not generalize to real-world visual sequences.
    *   **First-Person Pronoun Grounding Issues:** The lower grounding rates for first-person pronouns (largely due to dialogue) suggest an area for improvement in the grounding methodology.
    *   **Automatic Evaluation Challenges:** Automated metrics can be difficult to apply to visual storytelling, potentially penalizing creative narratives that deviate from the reference stories. The LLM-based metrics address this to some extent, but are also subject to biases.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The StoryReasoning dataset has the potential to become a benchmark for multi-frame visual storytelling systems. It can inspire new approaches for maintaining consistency and grounding in narrative generation. The interactive visualization tool can also promote broader engagement with the research.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**
    The paper presents a valuable resource and baseline for a challenging problem, which are likely to push the field forward. The systematic approach to data creation, entity identification and the novel way it combines current SOTA approaches into a single system are significant. However, the methods themselves are more improvements on existing ideas as opposed to paradigm shifts, which limits the score. Despite these considerations, the comprehensive approach and demonstrable improvement warrants a good rating.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Empirically evaluating commonsense intelligence in large language models with large-scale human judgments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10309v1)
- **Authors**: Tuan Dung Nguyen, Duncan J. Watts, Mark E. Whiting
- **Abstract**: Commonsense intelligence in machines is often assessed by static benchmarks that compare a model's output against human-prescribed correct labels. An important, albeit implicit, assumption of these labels is that they accurately capture what any human would think, effectively treating human common sense as homogeneous. However, recent empirical work has shown that humans vary enormously in what they consider commonsensical; thus what appears self-evident to one benchmark designer may not be so to another. Here, we propose a novel method for evaluating common sense in artificial intelligence (AI), specifically in large language models (LLMs), that incorporates empirically observed heterogeneity among humans by measuring the correspondence between a model's judgment and that of a human population. We first find that, when treated as independent survey respondents, most LLMs remain below the human median in their individual commonsense competence. Second, when used as simulators of a hypothetical population, LLMs correlate with real humans only modestly in the extent to which they agree on the same set of statements. In both cases, smaller, open-weight models are surprisingly more competitive than larger, proprietary frontier models. Our evaluation framework, which ties commonsense intelligence to its cultural basis, contributes to the growing call for adapting AI models to human collectivities that possess different, often incompatible, social stocks of knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating commonsense intelligence in large language models (LLMs). It argues that traditional benchmarks, which rely on static "correct" labels, fail to account for the significant heterogeneity in human commonsense reasoning.  Instead, the authors propose a novel evaluation framework that incorporates empirically observed human variation by measuring the correspondence between a model's judgments and those of a human population. They use two main approaches: (1) treating LLMs as independent survey respondents and comparing their "commonsensicality" (a measure combining consensus with the human majority and awareness of that majority opinion) to that of human participants, and (2) treating LLMs as simulators of hypothetical populations ("silicon samples") and assessing how well they reproduce aggregated human opinions. The results suggest that most LLMs perform below the human median in individual commonsense competence, and correlate only modestly with real human agreement patterns when used as population simulators.  Surprisingly, smaller, open-weight models sometimes outperform larger, proprietary models. The authors argue that their framework contributes to the call for aligning AI models with diverse, often incompatible, human knowledge systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in its novel evaluation framework for commonsense reasoning, which moves beyond simple accuracy metrics to incorporate human heterogeneity.  This is a significant departure from standard evaluation practices in the field. The idea of treating an LLM as a population simulator, and then comparing the statistical behavior of that simulation to human survey data, is also quite novel. The application of cultural competence or cultural consonance concepts to evaluating AI is another strength.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have important implications for AI alignment and the development of human-like AI.  By highlighting the limitations of current benchmarks and demonstrating the variability in human commonsense, the authors make a strong case for re-evaluating how we assess and develop these capabilities. Their work suggests that LLMs need to be more sensitive to cultural and contextual factors. The findings that smaller open-source models can be competitive on this metric is also valuable and potentially significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The paper employs a clearly defined methodology, leveraging a large-scale dataset of human judgments and analyzing the performance of multiple LLMs. Their metric, while complex, is well-justified.
    *   **Well-articulated argument:** The authors present a coherent and compelling argument for their approach, building on existing research on human heterogeneity and the limitations of current AI benchmarks.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its methodology, results, and conclusions, despite the complexity of the approach. Figures are helpful in understanding the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Specific Dataset:** The findings are limited by the dataset used, which mostly reflects the viewpoints of a specific population (US-based Mechanical Turk workers).  While the authors acknowledge this limitation and emphasize that the framework can be adapted to different populations, it's a crucial factor.  The generalizability of the results to other cultural contexts remains to be seen.
    *   **Complexity of the Metric:** The "commonsensicality" metric, while well-motivated, is somewhat complex. The need to combine a subjective judgement and an accurate prediction requires a careful balance. A simple accuracy measure is likely more straightforward to understand.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Qualitative Differences:** While the paper identifies some qualitative differences between human and LLM opinions (e.g., Gemini's association of common sense with figures of speech), this aspect could be explored in more depth.
    *   **Elo rating as a comparison:** LMSYS Arena is a measure of general appeal to humans, not necessarily a measure of objective common sense. A strong link is made, but is difficult to verify.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the direction of AI research by prompting a shift away from traditional benchmarks toward more nuanced and culturally sensitive evaluation metrics. It could also inspire further research on how to better align AI models with diverse human values and beliefs.

*The work appears well-written, thoroughly researched, and contributes a valuable perspective to a relevant discussion within the AI community. The significance of its contribution should inspire additional research, and for these reasons it deserves a significant score.*

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### SOS: A Shuffle Order Strategy for Data Augmentation in Industrial Human Activity Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10312v1)
- **Authors**: Anh Tuan Ha, Hoang Khang Phan, Thai Minh Tien Ngo, Anh Phan Truong, Nhat Tan Le
- **Abstract**: In the realm of Human Activity Recognition (HAR), obtaining high quality and variance data is still a persistent challenge due to high costs and the inherent variability of real-world activities. This study introduces a generation dataset by deep learning approaches (Attention Autoencoder and conditional Generative Adversarial Networks). Another problem that data heterogeneity is a critical challenge, one of the solutions is to shuffle the data to homogenize the distribution. Experimental results demonstrate that the random sequence strategy significantly improves classification performance, achieving an accuracy of up to 0.70 $\pm$ 0.03 and a macro F1 score of 0.64 $\pm$ 0.01. For that, disrupting temporal dependencies through random sequence reordering compels the model to focus on instantaneous recognition, thereby improving robustness against activity transitions. This approach not only broadens the effective training dataset but also offers promising avenues for enhancing HAR systems in complex, real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new data augmentation strategy called "Shuffle Order Strategy" (SOS) for improving the performance of Human Activity Recognition (HAR) models in industrial settings.  The core idea is to strategically shuffle the order of data sequences, disrupting temporal dependencies to force the model to focus on instantaneous recognition and improve robustness to activity transitions. The SOS strategy is combined with deep learning-based synthetic data generation using Attention Autoencoders (AAE) and Conditional Generative Adversarial Networks (CTGAN). The approach is evaluated on the OpenPack dataset, and the results indicate that the random sequence reordering in SOS significantly improves classification accuracy and F1 score compared to other data augmentation strategies, particularly when combined with data generated from CTGAN.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The paper proposes a relatively novel data augmentation technique, SOS, which focuses on reordering sequences rather than traditional methods of adding noise or synthetic data generation. The idea of disrupting temporal dependencies to force the model to learn more robust features is insightful.
    * **Methodology:** The paper employs well-established deep learning methods (AAE, CTGAN) for synthetic data generation and a Transformer-based model for HAR, demonstrating a good understanding of existing techniques.
    * **Experimental Results:** The experiments are well-designed and the results show significant improvement in performance (accuracy and F1 score) with the proposed approach compared to baselines. The analysis of why different reordering strategies perform differently provides valuable insights.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem (HAR in industrial settings) where data is often scarce and variable.  Improving HAR in these contexts has tangible benefits in workflow optimization and safety.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily based on one dataset (OpenPack).  While this dataset is relevant, the generalization ability of the proposed approach to other datasets or industrial settings remains unclear.
    * **Lack of Deep Dive on Optimal Shuffling:** The paper primarily investigates "random shuffling." However, it could have explored different types of shuffling strategies in more depth (e.g., shuffling within specific time windows, shuffling based on activity types, etc.).  This would have provided more insight into the optimal way to disrupt temporal dependencies.
    * **Limited Explanation of Why SOS works:** While the paper postulates that SOS works by forcing the model to focus on instantaneous recognition, the paper did not further support this. The discriminator analysis is minimal and lacks conclusive evidence. What features are being learned when SOS is applied? This is essential for the readers to better understand the workings of the proposed approach.
    * **Standard Deviation:** The AAE model shows a high standard deviation, indicating instability.
* **Significance:**
    * The paper provides a promising data augmentation strategy for HAR, especially in situations with limited data and high variability.  The SOS approach could be easily integrated with other HAR models and data augmentation techniques. The idea of strategically disrupting temporal dependencies could be applicable to other time-series classification problems.
* **Clarity and Presentation:**
    * The paper is generally well-written and easy to understand. The figures and tables are clear and provide helpful information.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective data augmentation strategy for HAR, demonstrated through well-designed experiments.  While the scope of evaluation is somewhat limited and more in-depth analysis of SOS would be beneficial, the results are significant and the approach has practical relevance.  The paper could serve as a valuable contribution to the field of HAR and time-series classification.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### J1: Incentivizing Thinking in LLM-as-a-Judge via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10320v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Whitehouse, Tianlu Wang, Ping Yu, Xian Li, Jason Weston, Ilia Kulikov, Swarnadeep Saha
- **Abstract**: The progress of AI is bottlenecked by the quality of evaluation, and powerful LLM-as-a-Judge models have proved to be a core solution. Improved judgment ability is enabled by stronger chain-of-thought reasoning, motivating the need to find the best recipes for training such models to think. In this work we introduce J1, a reinforcement learning approach to training such models. Our method converts both verifiable and non-verifiable prompts to judgment tasks with verifiable rewards that incentivize thinking and mitigate judgment bias. In particular, our approach outperforms all other existing 8B or 70B models when trained at those sizes, including models distilled from DeepSeek-R1. J1 also outperforms o1-mini, and even R1 on some benchmarks, despite training a smaller model.
