- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforcing the Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought with Diffusion Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10446v1)
- **Authors**: Zemin Huang, Zhiyang Chen, Zijun Wang, Tiancheng Li, Guo-Jun Qi
- **Abstract**: We introduce the \emph{Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought (DCoLT)}, a reasoning framework for diffusion language models. DCoLT treats each intermediate step in the reverse diffusion process as a latent "thinking" action and optimizes the entire reasoning trajectory to maximize the reward on the correctness of the final answer with outcome-based Reinforcement Learning (RL). Unlike traditional Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods that follow a causal, linear thinking process, DCoLT allows bidirectional, non-linear reasoning with no strict rule on grammatical correctness amid its intermediate steps of thought. We implement DCoLT on two representative Diffusion Language Models (DLMs). First, we choose SEDD as a representative continuous-time discrete diffusion model, where its concrete score derives a probabilistic policy to maximize the RL reward over the entire sequence of intermediate diffusion steps. We further consider the discrete-time masked diffusion language model -- LLaDA, and find that the order to predict and unmask tokens plays an essential role to optimize its RL action resulting from the ranking-based Unmasking Policy Module (UPM) defined by the Plackett-Luce model. Experiments on both math and code generation tasks show that using only public data and 16 H800 GPUs, DCoLT-reinforced DLMs outperform other DLMs trained by SFT or RL or even both. Notably, DCoLT-reinforced LLaDA boosts its reasoning accuracy by +9.8%, +5.7%, +11.4%, +19.5% on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diffusion Chain of Lateral Thought (DCoLT), a novel reasoning framework for diffusion language models (DLMs).  DCoLT treats each intermediate step in the reverse diffusion process as a latent "thinking" action and optimizes the entire reasoning trajectory using reinforcement learning (RL) based on the correctness of the final answer. Unlike Chain-of-Thought (CoT), DCoLT enables bidirectional, non-linear reasoning without strict grammatical constraints in the intermediate steps. The authors implemented DCoLT on two DLMs: SEDD (continuous-time) and LLaDA (discrete-time).  Experiments on math and code generation tasks demonstrated that DCoLT-reinforced DLMs outperform other DLMs trained with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or RL, even with limited public data.  Notably, DCoLT-reinforced LLaDA shows significant improvements in reasoning accuracy on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable approach to reasoning in language models.  The core idea of leveraging the intermediate steps of diffusion models as "thinking" actions and optimizing them via RL is novel. DCoLT addresses a key limitation of auto-regressive models, the strictly sequential reasoning process, allowing for more flexible and potentially creative exploration of solutions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The DCoLT framework introduces a new perspective on reasoning within DLMs, departing from the traditional CoT paradigm. It effectively repurposes the diffusion process for iterative reasoning.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The integration of RL with DLMs, especially the introduction of the Unmasking Policy Module (UPM) for LLaDA, appears technically well-executed.
*   **Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling. The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements compared to baseline DLMs and even some autoregressive models, particularly considering the limited training data used. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the UPM and highlight the effectiveness of the end-to-end RL approach. The comparison with SEDD and LLaDA helps show the general applicability of the DCoLT framework.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the DCoLT framework and its implementation in a clear and understandable manner.  The illustrations and diagrams aid in comprehension.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational demands of training DCoLT-reinforced DLMs. Generating multiple trajectories for RL and backpropagating gradients through numerous diffusion steps can be computationally expensive. While the experiments are performed on H800 GPUs, making it more affordable than older setups, the lack of clear resource requirements for other environments may limit the accessibility of the work.
*   **Generalizability:** The evaluation is primarily focused on math and code generation tasks, which have verifiable correctness criteria.  The applicability of DCoLT to more complex reasoning tasks, requiring subjective judgment or handling ambiguous information, is not explored, and this is admitted by the authors,
*   **Deeper Analysis of Lateral Thinking:** Although the paper frames the approach as "lateral thinking," there isn't a rigorous analysis of *how* DCoLT specifically promotes lateral thinking compared to simply optimizing the final result. There might be ways to quantify or characterize the diversity of reasoning trajectories, which would strengthen the claim of promoting lateral thought.
*   **Reward function engineering**: Using a hard reward function works reasonably well. Future work should also try to explore reward shaping that encourages more appropriate behavior.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases**: More specific examples of failure cases could provide useful insights about the limitations of DCoLT. A discussion of what prevents them from succeeding more is necessary.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to advance the field of reasoning in language models. DCoLT provides a novel and effective framework for training DLMs to perform complex reasoning tasks without explicit step-by-step supervision. This could lead to the development of more flexible, creative, and data-efficient reasoning models. However, more research is needed to explore its generalizability and address the computational concerns.

The improvement that DCoLT brings to several DLMs is also significant; while DLMs do not dominate the field of language models, their usefulness, and accessibility improve with frameworks such as DCoLT.

The paper also presents an effective strategy to bridge the gap between reasoning and diffusion models, a significant step forward.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach with strong empirical results. The DCoLT framework is a valuable contribution to the field of reasoning in language models. However, the limitations regarding computational cost, limited evaluation to specific tasks, and lack of deeper analysis of the "lateral thinking" claim prevent it from receiving a higher score. A critical weakness is the lack of demonstration of generalizability. It's important to note that while improvements were observed in many of the test datasets, this does not mean that the model will perform as robustly on other scenarios. Further work is also needed regarding reward engineering and failure analysis, both of which would benefit the potential of the model.


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Superposition Yields Robust Neural Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10465v1)
- **Authors**: Yizhou liu, Ziming Liu, Jeff Gore
- **Abstract**: The success of today's large language models (LLMs) depends on the observation that larger models perform better. However, the origin of this neural scaling law -- the finding that loss decreases as a power law with model size -- remains unclear. Starting from two empirical principles -- that LLMs represent more things than the model dimensions (widths) they have (i.e., representations are superposed), and that words or concepts in language occur with varying frequencies -- we constructed a toy model to study the loss scaling with model size. We found that when superposition is weak, meaning only the most frequent features are represented without interference, the scaling of loss with model size depends on the underlying feature frequency; if feature frequencies follow a power law, so does the loss. In contrast, under strong superposition, where all features are represented but overlap with each other, the loss becomes inversely proportional to the model dimension across a wide range of feature frequency distributions. This robust scaling behavior is explained geometrically: when many more vectors are packed into a lower dimensional space, the interference (squared overlaps) between vectors scales inversely with that dimension. We then analyzed four families of open-sourced LLMs and found that they exhibit strong superposition and quantitatively match the predictions of our toy model. The Chinchilla scaling law turned out to also agree with our results. We conclude that representation superposition is an important mechanism underlying the observed neural scaling laws. We anticipate that these insights will inspire new training strategies and model architectures to achieve better performance with less computation and fewer parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the origins of neural scaling laws in large language models (LLMs), focusing on the role of representation superposition. The authors propose that LLMs operate under a strong superposition regime, where the number of features (tokens, concepts) exceeds the model's dimensionality, leading to feature overlap.  They develop a toy model to study loss scaling as a function of model size, degree of superposition, and feature frequency distribution. The model reveals that strong superposition leads to robust inverse proportionality between loss and model dimension, independent of feature frequency distributions. In contrast, weak superposition results in scaling behavior that is highly sensitive to feature frequency distributions. Analysis of several LLM families suggests they exhibit strong superposition, aligning with the toy model's predictions and offering a potential explanation for observed neural scaling laws. They also show that the Chinchilla scaling law agrees with their findings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a fresh perspective on neural scaling laws by emphasizing the role of *representation superposition*. While superposition has been explored before, this work directly links it to the *robustness and universality* of scaling laws. The toy model provides a valuable tool for understanding how superposition and data structure interact to influence scaling behavior.
*   **Significance:** Explaining and predicting scaling laws is crucial for efficient LLM development. By identifying representation superposition as a key mechanism, the paper offers insights that could potentially guide the design of new training strategies or architectures that achieve better performance with fewer parameters. Linking the strong superposition to known LLMs provides strong evidence for its significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The development and analysis of the toy model is a clear strength. It's simple enough to be analytically tractable but complex enough to capture the essential dynamics.
    *   Connecting the model's predictions to observations from real LLMs significantly strengthens the paper's impact. The quantitative comparison adds credibility.
    *   The paper's discussion of potential implications for LLM training strategies is valuable, suggesting concrete avenues for future research.
    *   Clear explanations and organization throughout.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The toy model, while informative, is still a simplified representation of real LLMs. It's important to acknowledge the limitations of the model and avoid overstating the generality of its predictions.  Specifically, the omission of transformer layers and simplified loss function should be considered.
    *   The paper relies heavily on empirical observations from the toy model and shallow theoretical analysis. More rigorous analytical derivations would strengthen the results and improve confidence in the findings. In the section 5 discussion, the author acknowledges that solving the toy model is needed for the detailed questions.
    *   The claim that the findings suggest weight decay is not good is too strong.
    *   The analysis of data is less detailed than the discussion of the results. This makes assessing the accuracy of claims more difficult.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Shifting the focus of scaling law research towards representation-level mechanisms.
    *   Inspiring new architectures and training strategies that explicitly encourage representation superposition.
    *   Providing a framework for understanding and comparing different LLM designs.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper demonstrates significant novelty in linking representation superposition to the robustness of neural scaling laws. The toy model offers a valuable tool for understanding the interactions between superposition, data structure, and scaling behavior. The connection to real LLMs strengthens the impact of the findings. However, the limitations of the toy model and the reliance on empirical observations from it warrant a slightly lower score. It represents a good step in the right direction for researching the origins of scale but is not definitive.

### AI Agents vs. Agentic AI: A Conceptual Taxonomy, Applications and Challenge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10468v1)
- **Authors**: Ranjan Sapkota, Konstantinos I. Roumeliotis, Manoj Karkee
- **Abstract**: This study critically distinguishes between AI Agents and Agentic AI, offering a structured conceptual taxonomy, application mapping, and challenge analysis to clarify their divergent design philosophies and capabilities. We begin by outlining the search strategy and foundational definitions, characterizing AI Agents as modular systems driven by Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Image Models (LIMs) for narrow, task-specific automation. Generative AI is positioned as a precursor, with AI Agents advancing through tool integration, prompt engineering, and reasoning enhancements. In contrast, Agentic AI systems represent a paradigmatic shift marked by multi-agent collaboration, dynamic task decomposition, persistent memory, and orchestrated autonomy. Through a sequential evaluation of architectural evolution, operational mechanisms, interaction styles, and autonomy levels, we present a comparative analysis across both paradigms. Application domains such as customer support, scheduling, and data summarization are contrasted with Agentic AI deployments in research automation, robotic coordination, and medical decision support. We further examine unique challenges in each paradigm including hallucination, brittleness, emergent behavior, and coordination failure and propose targeted solutions such as ReAct loops, RAG, orchestration layers, and causal modeling. This work aims to provide a definitive roadmap for developing robust, scalable, and explainable AI agent and Agentic AI-driven systems. >AI Agents, Agent-driven, Vision-Language-Models, Agentic AI Decision Support System, Agentic-AI Applications
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "AI Agents vs. Agentic AI: A Conceptual Taxonomy, Applications and Challenges" provides a structured comparison between AI Agents and Agentic AI systems. It argues that while both build on Large Language Models (LLMs), they represent distinct paradigms. AI Agents are characterized as modular, task-specific systems, while Agentic AI involves multi-agent collaboration, dynamic task decomposition, and persistent memory. The paper establishes a taxonomy based on architecture, mechanisms, scope, and autonomy, and maps the application domains and challenges of each paradigm. It proposes solutions like Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and causal modeling to address issues like hallucination and coordination failure. Ultimately, the authors aim to provide a roadmap for developing robust, scalable, and explainable AI systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a useful conceptual framework for distinguishing AI Agents and Agentic AI. While the components (LLMs, tools, multi-agent systems) are known, the *explicit comparison* and *structured taxonomy* is a valuable contribution. The categorization of challenges specific to each paradigm and proposed solutions adds practical value. The paper synthesizes existing work, but its *clear distinction* between agent types and the proposed taxonomy constitutes a novel organization of ideas.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant need for clarity in the rapidly evolving field of AI. The rise of "AI Agents" and "Agentic AI" has led to ambiguous terminology. By providing a structured taxonomy and comparison, the paper can help researchers and practitioners design, evaluate, and deploy these systems more effectively.  The analysis of limitations also helps to guide future research. The identification and proposed solutions have the potential to inform the development of more robust and reliable agent-based systems, particularly in critical domains.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Definitions and Taxonomy:** The paper excels in providing clear definitions and a well-structured taxonomy for AI Agents and Agentic AI. This addresses the ambiguity in the field.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The analysis covers architecture, mechanisms, applications, and challenges, providing a well-rounded perspective.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper proposes actionable solutions to address limitations and outlines a roadmap for future development.
    *   **Relevant Application Mapping:** The identification of specific application domains for each paradigm is highly valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The paper primarily focuses on conceptual analysis and literature synthesis.  It would be strengthened by including empirical evidence or case studies demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed solutions.
    *   **Oversimplification Potential:** The categorization, while helpful, could oversimplify the nuances within each paradigm. There is a risk of creating artificial boundaries between systems that exist on a continuum.
    *   **Future is speculative:** Roadmap includes some speculations of the future, which might or might not be valid.
    *   **Limited detail on Generative Agents:** Despite being mentioned, generative agents are not discussed in detail.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has a high potential to influence the field. The taxonomy can be adopted as a standard framework for classifying agent-based systems. The identified challenges and solutions can guide future research directions. The analysis can inform the development of more robust and reliable AI systems, particularly in safety-critical applications. It also has educational value in training new researchers and practitioners.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper is a *valuable contribution* to the field by providing a *clear and structured framework* for understanding AI Agents and Agentic AI. Its strength lies in the synthesis of existing knowledge and the articulation of key differences and challenges. However, it's limited by a *lack of empirical validation* and the *potential for oversimplification*.  Despite these limitations, the paper is a *significant step forward* in clarifying the agentic landscape and has a *high potential* to influence future research and development in this rapidly evolving area.

- **Classification**: cs.AI

### Large Language Models for Cancer Communication: Evaluating Linguistic Quality, Safety, and Accessibility in Generative AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10472v1)
- **Authors**: Agnik Saha, Victoria Churchill, Anny D. Rodriguez, Ugur Kursuncu, Muhammed Y. Idris
- **Abstract**: Effective communication about breast and cervical cancers remains a persistent health challenge, with significant gaps in public understanding of cancer prevention, screening, and treatment, potentially leading to delayed diagnoses and inadequate treatments. This study evaluates the capabilities and limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating accurate, safe, and accessible cancer-related information to support patient understanding. We evaluated five general-purpose and three medical LLMs using a mixed-methods evaluation framework across linguistic quality, safety and trustworthiness, and communication accessibility and affectiveness. Our approach utilized quantitative metrics, qualitative expert ratings, and statistical analysis using Welch's ANOVA, Games-Howell, and Hedges' g. Our results show that general-purpose LLMs produced outputs of higher linguistic quality and affectiveness, while medical LLMs demonstrate greater communication accessibility. However, medical LLMs tend to exhibit higher levels of potential harm, toxicity, and bias, reducing their performance in safety and trustworthiness. Our findings indicate a duality between domain-specific knowledge and safety in health communications. The results highlight the need for intentional model design with targeted improvements, particularly in mitigating harm and bias, and improving safety and affectiveness. This study provides a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs for cancer communication, offering critical insights for improving AI-generated health content and informing future development of accurate, safe, and accessible digital health tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Large Language Models for Cancer Communication: Evaluating Linguistic Quality, Safety, and Accessibility in Generative AI" investigates the capabilities and limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs), both general-purpose and medical-specific, in generating accurate, safe, and accessible information related to breast and cervical cancers. The study utilizes a mixed-methods evaluation framework encompassing linguistic quality, safety and trustworthiness, communication accessibility, and affectiveness.  The authors evaluate five general-purpose LLMs (Llama 3, Gemma, Alpaca, Mistral, Vicuna) and three specialized medical LLMs (MedAlpaca, BioMistral, Meditron) using quantitative metrics, qualitative expert ratings, and statistical analyses. The results highlight a duality, where general-purpose LLMs excel in linguistic quality and affectiveness, while medical LLMs demonstrate greater communication accessibility but also exhibit higher potential for harm, toxicity, and bias. The study emphasizes the need for targeted model design improvements, particularly in mitigating harm and bias, and improving safety and affectiveness for AI-generated health content.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant and relatively unexplored area: the systematic evaluation of LLMs for cancer communication. While there have been studies evaluating LLMs in healthcare, the specific focus on breast and cervical cancer, with a mixed-methods approach across linguistic quality, safety, accessibility, and affectiveness, adds novelty. The comparison between general-purpose and medical LLMs in this specific context is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The research is highly significant because effective cancer communication is crucial for improving public health outcomes and reducing disparities. The findings reveal the potential of LLMs to enhance access to health information but also highlight critical risks associated with their use, especially regarding safety and bias in medical-specific models. The paper's insights can inform the development and deployment of safer and more effective AI-driven health communication tools.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The mixed-methods approach, combining quantitative metrics, qualitative expert ratings, and statistical analysis, provides a robust and multi-faceted evaluation.
    *   **Direct Comparison of LLMs:**  The direct comparison of general-purpose and medical LLMs allows for a clear understanding of their relative strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Focus on Accessibility and Safety:** The emphasis on accessibility and safety considerations is particularly important in the context of health communication, where vulnerable populations may be disproportionately affected by misleading or biased information.
    *   **Clear Presentation of Results:** The paper effectively presents its findings with clear tables, figures, and statistical analyses, enhancing the readability and interpretability of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitations:**  While the dataset is domain-specific, the paper acknowledges potential topic or language distribution biases within the dataset.
    *   **Model Selection:** The choice of LLMs might be constrained by availability and resource considerations (limited to open-source models with <8B parameters).  The performance might differ with larger, proprietary models.
    *   **Contextual Factors:**  The study does not fully capture the complexity of real-world patient interactions. Factors like cultural context, language preference, and patient-specific health literacy could influence the effectiveness of LLM-generated communication.  The paper mentions that future research could incorporate these factors.
    *   **Generalizability:**  While focused on breast and cervical cancer, the paper could benefit from discussing the generalizability of the findings to other health communication domains or specific populations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in AI-driven health communication by highlighting the importance of safety, accessibility, and multi-faceted evaluation frameworks. It can also guide the development of more responsible AI tools for patient education, preventive care, and clinical decision support. The findings may also inform policy decisions regarding the regulation and use of AI in healthcare.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by systematically evaluating LLMs in a crucial health communication domain. While it has some limitations regarding dataset biases, model selection, and contextual factors, its robust methodology, direct comparisons, and focus on safety and accessibility make it a valuable contribution to the field. The findings are relevant for researchers, developers, and policymakers involved in AI and healthcare.

### Fine-tuning Diffusion Policies with Backpropagation Through Diffusion Timesteps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10482v1)
- **Authors**: Ningyuan Yang, Jiaxuan Gao, Feng Gao, Yi Wu, Chao Yu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion policies, widely adopted in decision-making scenarios such as robotics, gaming and autonomous driving, are capable of learning diverse skills from demonstration data due to their high representation power. However, the sub-optimal and limited coverage of demonstration data could lead to diffusion policies that generate sub-optimal trajectories and even catastrophic failures. While reinforcement learning (RL)-based fine-tuning has emerged as a promising solution to address these limitations, existing approaches struggle to effectively adapt Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to diffusion models. This challenge stems from the computational intractability of action likelihood estimation during the denoising process, which leads to complicated optimization objectives. In our experiments starting from randomly initialized policies, we find that online tuning of Diffusion Policies demonstrates much lower sample efficiency compared to directly applying PPO on MLP policies (MLP+PPO). To address these challenges, we introduce NCDPO, a novel framework that reformulates Diffusion Policy as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy. By treating each denoising step as a differentiable transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, NCDPO enables tractable likelihood evaluation and gradient backpropagation through all diffusion timesteps. Our experiments demonstrate that NCDPO achieves sample efficiency comparable to MLP+PPO when training from scratch, outperforming existing methods in both sample efficiency and final performance across diverse benchmarks, including continuous robot control and multi-agent game scenarios. Furthermore, our experimental results show that our method is robust to the number denoising timesteps in the Diffusion Policy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Noise-Conditioned Diffusion Policy Optimization (NCDPO), a new reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm designed for fine-tuning diffusion policies.  Diffusion policies are effective for decision-making tasks but can suffer from suboptimal performance due to limitations in the demonstration data they are initially trained on. Existing RL fine-tuning methods, like DPPO, struggle with sample efficiency because they enlarge the action space by treating the denoising process as a low-level Markov Decision Process (MDP). NCDPO addresses this by reformulating the denoising process as a noise-conditioned deterministic policy, enabling tractable likelihood evaluation and gradient backpropagation through diffusion timesteps (BPDT). The authors demonstrate that NCDPO achieves comparable sample efficiency to directly applying Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) on MLP policies when training from scratch, and outperforms existing methods in sample efficiency and final performance on continuous robot control and multi-agent game benchmarks. The paper also shows that NCDPO's performance is robust to the number of denoising timesteps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the reformulation of the diffusion policy denoising process. By treating each denoising step as a deterministic transformation conditioned on pre-sampled noise, NCDPO avoids the action space expansion inherent in methods like DPPO. This is a significant departure and provides a more computationally tractable way to fine-tune diffusion policies with RL. The use of BPDT is not entirely new, but its effective application within this reformulated framework contributes to the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The primary significance of this work stems from its practical impact on RL fine-tuning of diffusion policies. By improving sample efficiency and final performance, NCDPO makes it more feasible to adapt pre-trained diffusion models to new environments and tasks. The paper's results on challenging benchmarks, including robot control and multi-agent settings, demonstrate the potential of the approach to address real-world decision-making problems. The robustness to the number of denoising steps is also important, as it provides flexibility in implementation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly identifies the sample efficiency challenge in RL fine-tuning of diffusion policies using existing methods.
    *   Well-motivated approach: The NCDPO framework is logically derived from the analysis of the limitations of DPPO.
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper provides convincing experimental evidence demonstrating the superiority of NCDPO over existing methods on diverse benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies: The ablation study showing robustness to the number of denoising timesteps provides useful insights.
    *   The code will be available, helping reproducibility

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mainly compares with DPPO but not with other RL methods for Diffusion Policies. Comparisons to other competitive RL methods designed to work with complex function approximators might strengthen the argument that NCDPO is a particularly well-suited method for *diffusion policies*, as opposed to just a generally effective RL algorithm.
    *   While the paper demonstrates strong performance, the theoretical understanding of why NCDPO performs better is somewhat limited. The claim that BPDT leading to more accurate gradient estimates relies on intuition. More rigorous analysis would be beneficial.
    *   The dependence on careful hyperparameter tuning is mentioned. While hyperparameters are provided, a more in-depth discussion of the sensitivity of NCDPO to different hyperparameter choices could be valuable.
    *   The focus is primarily on simulated environments. The lack of real-world robotics experiments is a limitation, although the Robomimic experiments partially address this. Sim-to-real transfer is important for assessing the practical applicability of the algorithm.

*   **Potential Influence:** NCDPO has the potential to become a standard approach for RL fine-tuning of diffusion policies. Its improved sample efficiency and performance compared to existing methods, coupled with its robustness and general applicability, make it a valuable contribution to the field. The clear presentation and empirical validation should encourage further research and adoption.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated algorithm for a practically important problem. The strengths of the empirical results and the novelty of the approach outweigh the limitations. The clear problem definition, logical approach, and solid validation warrant a high score.

### Campus AI vs Commercial AI: A Late-Breaking Study on How LLM As-A-Service Customizations Shape Trust and Usage Patterns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10490v1)
- **Authors**: Leon Hannig, Annika Bush, Meltem Aksoy, Steffen Becker, Greta Ontrup
- **Abstract**: As the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) by students, lecturers and researchers becomes more prevalent, universities - like other organizations - are pressed to develop coherent AI strategies. LLMs as-a-Service (LLMaaS) offer accessible pre-trained models, customizable to specific (business) needs. While most studies prioritize data, model, or infrastructure adaptations (e.g., model fine-tuning), we focus on user-salient customizations, like interface changes and corporate branding, which we argue influence users' trust and usage patterns. This study serves as a functional prequel to a large-scale field study in which we examine how students and employees at a German university perceive and use their institution's customized LLMaaS compared to ChatGPT. The goals of this prequel are to stimulate discussions on psychological effects of LLMaaS customizations and refine our research approach through feedback. Our forthcoming findings will deepen the understanding of trust dynamics in LLMs, providing practical guidance for organizations considering LLMaaS deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a late-breaking study design aimed at understanding how customizations in Large Language Models as a Service (LLMaaS) affect user trust, perception, and usage patterns compared to commercial LLMs like ChatGPT.  The authors focus particularly on user-salient customizations (e.g., interface branding) rather than deeper technical adaptations (like model fine-tuning). They hypothesize that customizations, even those seemingly superficial, can significantly influence user trust and behavior due to psychological cues. The study plans to investigate these effects within a German university setting, comparing user responses to a university-branded LLMaaS versus ChatGPT.  Hypotheses are formulated around the impact of customization on trust, hallucination perception, privacy concerns, and sustainable AI usage. The study aims to provide practical guidance for organizations considering LLMaaS deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's central premise – investigating the *psychological* impact of LLMaaS customizations beyond mere technical performance – has some novelty.  While much research focuses on fine-tuning or security aspects, this paper aims to understand the user-experience component of seemingly cosmetic changes. The emphasis on corporate branding as a trust cue is a relatively unexplored area within the LLM context.

*   **Significance:**  The topic is relevant and timely. As LLMs become pervasive in education and professional settings, understanding user perceptions and trust is crucial for responsible AI adoption. The focus on LLMaaS is also practically significant, as organizations are increasingly exploring this deployment model.  The identification of factors beyond technical performance is essential for ensuring user adoption and avoiding overreliance or misuse of AI.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly defined research question:** The RQ is precise and focuses on a relatively unexplored area.
    *   **Sound theoretical grounding:**  The hypotheses are well-motivated by psychological theories of trust, cue utilization, and technology acceptance. The discussion of organizational trust as a moderator is particularly insightful.
    *   **Practical implications:**  The study's findings could directly inform the design and deployment of LLMaaS in organizations, leading to better user experiences and more appropriate trust levels.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The inclusion of aspects like sustainability concerns is commendable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Preliminary nature:**  As a late-breaking study, the paper presents only the study design and hypotheses. There are no empirical results or findings to evaluate. This significantly limits the assessment of its true impact.
    *   **Generalizability Concerns:** The planned study is limited to a single German university. Cultural and institutional factors could influence the results, making it difficult to generalize to other contexts.
    *   **Limited Focus on Customization Types:** The paper concentrates primarily on interface branding. While branding is important, other customization features, such as the availability of different model options, could also have a significant impact on trust and usage patterns.

*   **Overall Assessment:**  While the research questions and hypotheses are promising, the paper is preliminary and lacks empirical validation. The design is adequate, but the generalizability is a concern. The paper identifies an important, but not groundbreaking, area of research. It has the potential to make a modest contribution if the actual study results confirm the hypotheses and provide actionable insights.

**Score: 5.5**

**Rationale:** The score of 5.5 reflects the paper's potential. It presents a novel angle on a relevant topic with sound theoretical grounding. However, the absence of results and the limitations of the study design (single university, narrow customization focus) temper the assessment. It's a solid start but needs empirical data and a broader scope to demonstrate significant impact. The significance hinges on results that have not yet been presented.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 5/10

### CL-RAG: Bridging the Gap in Retrieval-Augmented Generation with Curriculum Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10493v1)
- **Authors**: Shaohan Wang, Licheng Zhang, Zheren Fu, Zhendong Mao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an effective method to enhance the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Existing methods focus on optimizing the retriever or generator in the RAG system by directly utilizing the top-k retrieved documents. However, the documents effectiveness are various significantly across user queries, i.e. some documents provide valuable knowledge while others totally lack critical information. It hinders the retriever and generator's adaptation during training. Inspired by human cognitive learning, curriculum learning trains models using samples progressing from easy to difficult, thus enhancing their generalization ability, and we integrate this effective paradigm to the training of the RAG system. In this paper, we propose a multi-stage Curriculum Learning based RAG system training framework, named CL-RAG. We first construct training data with multiple difficulty levels for the retriever and generator separately through sample evolution. Then, we train the model in stages based on the curriculum learning approach, thereby optimizing the overall performance and generalization of the RAG system more effectively. Our CL-RAG framework demonstrates consistent effectiveness across four open-domain QA datasets, achieving performance gains of 2% to 4% over multiple advanced methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CL-RAG, a novel Curriculum Learning-based framework for training Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Recognizing that the effectiveness of retrieved documents varies significantly, the authors propose a multi-stage training approach inspired by human cognitive learning. CL-RAG involves constructing training data with varying difficulty levels for both the retriever and generator. The generator is trained using progressively more challenging document sets (easy, common, hard) constructed through rewriting techniques (query enhancement and counterfactual noise). The retriever is trained to align with a well-trained generator (RALM) using a hierarchical training strategy that focuses on distinguishing documents with increasingly subtle quality differences. Experiments on four open-domain question-answering datasets demonstrate improved performance compared to advanced baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of curriculum learning to RAG systems, especially for the *joint* optimization of both the retriever and generator. Existing RAG methods primarily focus on optimizing either the retriever or the generator independently, or they don't explicitly address the varying quality of retrieved documents during training through a curriculum approach. The strategy of constructing data with varying difficulty levels through rewriting and the hierarchical retriever training are also contributions, though they build upon established techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the generalization and robustness of RAG systems. By explicitly addressing the varying quality of retrieved documents during training, CL-RAG can lead to models that are more resilient to noise and better equipped to utilize diverse information sources. The experimental results, showing consistent performance gains across multiple datasets, support this claim. The findings suggest that curriculum learning is a promising direction for future RAG research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of varying document quality and its impact on RAG training.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The use of curriculum learning is well-motivated by both cognitive science principles and previous work in deep learning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, including comparisons with multiple strong baselines and ablation studies to assess the contribution of different components.
    *   **Demonstrated Effectiveness:** The results demonstrate consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets.
    *   **Addresses a significant challenge:** The paper tackled a critical issue for RAG systems and made real progress towards improving the robustness of the systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the application of curriculum learning to RAG is novel, many of the individual techniques (data augmentation, rewriting) are well-established.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework introduces several hyperparameters and training stages, potentially increasing the complexity of training RAG systems.
    *   **Limited Generalization Analysis:** While the paper shows performance improvements, a more in-depth analysis of the generalization capabilities of CL-RAG would be valuable. For example, assessing performance on out-of-domain datasets or datasets with more complex noise patterns.
    *   **Method limitations:** The method depends on the existence of a ground truth golden document for initializing the "easy" difficulty level, which may not be available for all tasks and could be a limitation for some RAG applications.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on RAG systems by highlighting the importance of curriculum learning and document quality. It may inspire the development of more sophisticated curriculum learning strategies and data augmentation techniques for RAG.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by introducing curriculum learning to RAG systems. While some of the individual techniques are not entirely new, their combination and application to this specific problem domain are significant. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and the consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets strengthen the paper's findings. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the increased complexity of the framework prevent a higher score.

### Can You Really Trust Code Copilots? Evaluating Large Language Models from a Code Security Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10494v1)
- **Authors**: Yutao Mou, Xiao Deng, Yuxiao Luo, Shikun Zhang, Wei Ye
- **Abstract**: Code security and usability are both essential for various coding assistant applications driven by large language models (LLMs). Current code security benchmarks focus solely on single evaluation task and paradigm, such as code completion and generation, lacking comprehensive assessment across dimensions like secure code generation, vulnerability repair and discrimination. In this paper, we first propose CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark covering various tasks such as code completion, vulnerability repair, vulnerability detection and classification, for comprehensive evaluation of LLM code security. Besides, we developed VC-Judge, an improved judgment model that aligns closely with human experts and can review LLM-generated programs for vulnerabilities in a more efficient and reliable way. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 20 proprietary and open-source LLMs. Overall, while most LLMs identify vulnerable codes well, they still tend to generate insecure codes and struggle with recognizing specific vulnerability types and performing repairs. Extensive experiments and qualitative analyses reveal key challenges and optimization directions, offering insights for future research in LLM code security.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can You Really Trust Code Copilots? Evaluating Large Language Models from a Code Security Perspective" introduces CoV-Eval, a multi-task benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the code security of Large Language Models (LLMs). CoV-Eval includes tasks such as code completion, vulnerability repair, vulnerability detection, and vulnerability classification, spanning 18 vulnerability types across different programming languages. The authors also present VC-Judge, an improved LLM-based judgment model designed to provide more reliable vulnerability assessment of LLM-generated code. They evaluate 20 proprietary and open-source LLMs using CoV-Eval, revealing that while LLMs can often identify vulnerabilities, they still tend to generate insecure code and struggle with vulnerability repair and classification. The paper concludes by discussing challenges and optimization directions for improving LLM code security.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the CoV-Eval benchmark and the VC-Judge model. While individual code security evaluation datasets existed before, CoV-Eval distinguishes itself by offering a multi-task approach that considers various aspects of code security, such as code generation, vulnerability repair, and detection. VC-Judge, as an LLM-based evaluator, also represents a step forward from relying solely on traditional static analysis tools. Combining the benchmark and the improved evaluation method significantly increases the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its timely focus on a critical aspect of LLM adoption in software development: code security. The widespread use of code copilots like GitHub Copilot necessitates a thorough understanding of the security implications. By providing a benchmark and a more reliable evaluation method, the paper contributes to the development of more secure and trustworthy LLM-based coding assistants. The findings that LLMs often generate vulnerable code, despite their detection capabilities, highlight a crucial gap that needs to be addressed through further research and development. The results point towards some key areas for improvement and have actionable implications in industry.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The CoV-Eval benchmark provides a more comprehensive evaluation of LLM code security than existing single-task datasets.
    *   VC-Judge offers improved alignment with human expertise, leading to more reliable vulnerability assessment.
    *   The paper presents a thorough empirical analysis of 20 LLMs, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   The paper identifies key challenges and optimization directions for improving LLM code security.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o for data synthesis in the Vul-Evol framework might introduce biases and limit the diversity of the generated code scenarios.
    *   While VC-Judge improves alignment with human experts, it still has limitations, and its effectiveness might vary depending on the complexity of the vulnerability.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the specific types of vulnerabilities that LLMs struggle with most and the underlying reasons for these struggles.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM code security by:
    *   Providing a benchmark and evaluation method for comparing different LLMs.
    *   Guiding the development of more secure LLM-based coding assistants.
    *   Highlighting key challenges and optimization directions for improving LLM code security.
    *   Inspiring the creation of more sophisticated LLM-based vulnerability detection and repair tools.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**

CoV-Eval fills a gap in the current landscape of code security evaluation by offering a multi-faceted approach. VC-Judge is a necessary improvement that moves the field closer to reliable autmated evaluation of vulnerabilities in LLM-generated code.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper is well-written, technically sound, and addresses a relevant problem in the field. While there's room for improvement regarding the diversity of the generated code scenarios and a more detailed analysis of specific vulnerability types, the CoV-Eval benchmark and VC-Judge model represent significant contributions to the evaluation of LLM code security, and will most likely be the go-to resource for evaluating vulnerability detection and generation in the near future, setting a new standard in the field. Therefore a strong 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### RouteNator: A Router-Based Multi-Modal Architecture for Generating Synthetic Training Data for Function Calling LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10495v1)
- **Authors**: Vibha Belavadi, Tushar Vatsa, Dewang Sultania, Suhas Suresha, Ishita Verma, Cheng Chen, Tracy Holloway King, Michael Friedrich
- **Abstract**: This paper addresses fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for function calling tasks when real user interaction data is unavailable. In digital content creation tools, where users express their needs through natural language queries that must be mapped to API calls, the lack of real-world task-specific data and privacy constraints for training on it necessitate synthetic data generation. Existing approaches to synthetic data generation fall short in diversity and complexity, failing to replicate real-world data distributions and leading to suboptimal performance after LLM fine-tuning. We present a novel router-based architecture that leverages domain resources like content metadata and structured knowledge graphs, along with text-to-text and vision-to-text language models to generate high-quality synthetic training data. Our architecture's flexible routing mechanism enables synthetic data generation that matches observed real-world distributions, addressing a fundamental limitation of traditional approaches. Evaluation on a comprehensive set of real user queries demonstrates significant improvements in both function classification accuracy and API parameter selection. Models fine-tuned with our synthetic data consistently outperform traditional approaches, establishing new benchmarks for function calling tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RouteNator, a novel architecture for generating synthetic training data to improve the function calling capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). Function calling is the task of mapping natural language user requests to API calls, which is important for applications that use LLMs to control design tools, but real-world data for training these models is scarce due to privacy constraints. RouteNator addresses this scarcity by generating diverse and realistic synthetic data, integrating content metadata, knowledge graphs, and multi-modal (text and image) language models. The architecture uses a weighted router to direct query generation requests to different specialized prompt templates based on population-level statistics, length of query, and content requirements. Experiments demonstrate that LLMs fine-tuned with RouteNator's synthetic data outperform those trained with traditional synthetic data generation methods. The improvements are seen in function call classification accuracy and API parameter selection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integrated approach to synthetic data generation. While individual components like knowledge graphs, metadata, and vision-to-text models have been used before, RouteNator's router-based architecture, which combines these elements in a statistically driven and adaptive way to improve function call accuracy, represents a unique contribution. The novel router, incorporating multi-modal generation pathways and using population statistics to guide generation, is a key differentiator.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very important problem: the lack of real-world data for function calling fine-tuning of LLMs, particularly in scenarios constrained by privacy. Synthetic data generation is thus crucial, and improving the realism and diversity of this data is directly linked to enhanced downstream LLM performance. The reported improvements in function classification accuracy and API parameter selection are significant and demonstrate the practical value of the proposed approach. Specifically, the application of a weighted routing mechanisms using population-level statistics and integration of multi-modal language model can allow LLMs to generate the kind of diversity of data (real-world query) that can make it more efficient when fine-tuned to a function calling LLM,
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and the limitations of existing synthetic data generation methods.
    *   The proposed architecture is well-defined and explained, with a clear description of each component.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating improvements over baseline methods.
    *   The data diversity analysis (word count, content type, and keyword position) provide insight into how RouteNator improves data quality.
    *   The paper offers a strong blend of data diversity as well as maintaining realism.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper mentions privacy constraints motivating synthetic data generation, it doesn't delve deeply into the specifics of the privacy techniques used within the architecture or the guarantees it provides.
    *   The paper focuses predominantly on a single application domain (digital content creation). The generalizability of RouteNator to other domains is not thoroughly explored.
    *   A comparison of system memory utilization is good to point out the effectiveness of the system but it could be strengthen further by comparing against some open-source baseline or a comparison chart that includes more detailed hardware configurations of each machine.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a robust method for generating high-quality synthetic training data for function calling LLMs, and to drive the research on multi-modal architectures and statistically-driven data generation for LLMs. The integration of diverse data sources and prompt templates to improve realism and diversity is a promising direction.

**Score:** 8/10

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of synthetic data generation for LLMs. The RouteNator architecture is well-designed, and the experimental results are compelling. Although the paper has some weaknesses (lack of detail on privacy and generalizability), the strengths and potential influence of the work justify a strong score. The improvements in data realism and diversity, combined with the reported gains in downstream task performance, suggest that RouteNator can provide a practical and effective solution to the lack of real-world training data for function calling LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### S3C2 Summit 2024-09: Industry Secure Software Supply Chain Summit
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10538v1)
- **Authors**: Imranur Rahman, Yasemin Acar, Michel Cukier, William Enck, Christian Kastner, Alexandros Kapravelos, Dominik Wermke, Laurie Williams
- **Abstract**: While providing economic and software development value, software supply chains are only as strong as their weakest link. Over the past several years, there has been an exponential increase in cyberattacks, specifically targeting vulnerable links in critical software supply chains. These attacks disrupt the day-to-day functioning and threaten the security of nearly everyone on the internet, from billion-dollar companies and government agencies to hobbyist open-source developers. The ever-evolving threat of software supply chain attacks has garnered interest from the software industry and the US government in improving software supply chain security. On September 20, 2024, three researchers from the NSF-backed Secure Software Supply Chain Center (S3C2) conducted a Secure Software Supply Chain Summit with a diverse set of 12 practitioners from 9 companies. The goals of the Summit were to: (1) to enable sharing between individuals from different companies regarding practical experiences and challenges with software supply chain security, (2) to help form new collaborations, (3) to share our observations from our previous summits with industry, and (4) to learn about practitioners' challenges to inform our future research direction. The summit consisted of discussions of six topics relevant to the companies represented, including updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, building infrastructure, large language models, and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper summarizes the S3C2 Summit held in September 2024, focusing on secure software supply chains. It details discussions among 12 practitioners from 9 companies about key challenges and practices related to: updating vulnerable dependencies, component and container choice, malicious commits, build infrastructure, large language models (LLMs), and reducing entire classes of vulnerabilities.  The paper highlights the current state of practice for each topic, open questions raised during the summit, and key takeaways. It covers issues such as SBOM management, the limitations of relying on automated scores, the difficulty of detecting malicious commits, build infrastructure security, the nascent field of securing LLMs in the software supply chain, and strategies for reducing vulnerability classes. The paper reports industry's struggle and solutions in software supply chain security that may inspire more research in the area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its *capture of industry perspectives* on critical software supply chain security challenges *at a specific point in time*. While many of the individual topics discussed are well-known in the field, this paper provides a snapshot of how practitioners are currently grappling with them. This aspect of industry perspective makes the paper interesting in its time, but also the paper will serve as a historical document after a longer period.  The inclusion of LLMs as a supply chain risk is relatively new and a noteworthy addition. However, the paper primarily summarizes existing knowledge and discussions rather than presenting new technical solutions or research findings.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the paper resides in several aspects. First, it is in *bridging* the gap between research and practice by presenting a distilled view of the problems and practical considerations that industry faces. Second, it serves as a *source of research questions*. The open questions highlighted at the end of each section directly point to areas where further research is needed. Third, It may also serve as a reference point for future studies assessing the evolution of software supply chain security practices. However, the *lack of concrete solutions or actionable guidance* for practitioners limits its immediate impact. The Chatham House rule hinders the ability to assess the relative priorities assigned by different attendees to each point raised. The absence of vendor's perspective on some topics may have affected the comprehensive view of the software supply chain, limiting the importance of the paper.

* **Strengths:**
    * Provides a structured overview of key software supply chain security challenges.
    * Captures industry perspectives, offering valuable insights into real-world practices and pain points.
    * Identifies open research questions, stimulating further investigation in critical areas.
    * Timely discussion of emerging threats related to LLMs.
    * Discussion of security vs. productivity trade-offs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Lacks novel technical contributions or concrete solutions.
    * Primarily a summary of discussions, rather than an in-depth analysis or empirical study.
    * Relies on anecdotal evidence without quantitative data or rigorous evaluation.
    * The Chatham House rule restricts the level of detail and attribution.
    * Limited actionable guidance for practitioners.
    * The selection of companies attending may bias the report as a representative view of the industry.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is most likely to influence academic research by highlighting relevant problems for investigation. It also informs other industry practitioners about the state of the field. Its influence on immediate practice is limited due to the lack of concrete solutions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is valuable as a summary of current thinking on software supply chain security, but its lack of originality and reliance on anecdotal evidence constrain its overall impact. The open questions at the end of each section enhance its value by pointing researchers to areas where further investigation is needed. The focus on emerging LLM-related threats is a further benefit. However, it is a distillation of existing knowledge and concerns rather than a novel contribution. The lack of vendor's view may limit the significance and importance of the paper.

### Exploring Implicit Visual Misunderstandings in Multimodal Large Language Models through Attention Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10541v1)
- **Authors**: Pengfei Wang, Guohai Xu, Weinong Wang, Junjie Yang, Jie Lou, Yunhua Xue
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements have enhanced the capability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to comprehend multi-image information. However, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate answer correctness, overlooking whether models genuinely comprehend the visual input. To address this, we define implicit visual misunderstanding (IVM), where MLLMs provide correct answers without fully comprehending the visual input. Through our analysis, we decouple the visual and textual modalities within the causal attention module, revealing that attention distribution increasingly converges on the image associated with the correct answer as the network layers deepen. This insight leads to the introduction of a scale-agnostic metric, \textit{attention accuracy}, and a novel benchmark for quantifying IVMs. Attention accuracy directly evaluates the model's visual understanding via internal mechanisms, remaining robust to positional biases for more reliable assessments. Furthermore, we extend our approach to finer granularities and demonstrate its effectiveness in unimodal scenarios, underscoring its versatility and generalizability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Exploring Implicit Visual Misunderstandings in Multimodal Large Language Models through Attention Analysis":

**Summary:**

This paper tackles the problem of "implicit visual misunderstandings" (IVMs) in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). IVMs occur when an MLLM provides a correct answer to a question, but doesn't genuinely understand the relevant visual input, potentially relying on shortcuts or memorized associations instead. The authors analyze the causal attention module in MLLMs, decoupling visual and textual modalities to reveal that attention distribution converges on the image associated with the correct answer as layers deepen. Based on this observation, they introduce a scale-agnostic metric called "attention accuracy" and a novel benchmark, the "Single Target Multimodal Evaluation (STME)", to quantify IVMs. The authors demonstrate that attention accuracy is a robust way to evaluate the model's visual understanding and is effective even in unimodal scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its definition of IVMs and the proposed methodology to measure them using attention analysis. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on answer correctness (explicit errors), while this work attempts to get inside the "black box" and assess whether the model's internal mechanisms truly align with correct visual understanding. The STME benchmark, while containing a finite set of tasks, is a new resource specifically designed to highlight IVMs. The application of attention analysis, particularly decoupling modalities, is also novel within the specific context of diagnosing visual reasoning in MLLMs. The scale-agnostic "attention accuracy" metric is a reasonable and potentially valuable contribution for comparing models. The extension of the method to finer granularities and unimodal tasks shows good potential for future research directions.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant problem with MLLMs.  Simply achieving high accuracy on benchmark datasets isn't enough if the models aren't actually "seeing" or "understanding" in a meaningful way. IVMs can lead to unpredictable failures in real-world applications where models encounter situations outside their training distribution.  By providing a way to quantify IVMs, the paper opens the door to developing training techniques that promote genuine visual understanding and improve model robustness. The development of STME is also significant, as it provides the dataset for consistent measurement of visual understanding in various models. The insights gained from the attention analysis could also influence architecture design, making MLLMs more interpretable and reliable.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The concept of IVMs is clearly defined and well-motivated.
*   **Methodological Rigor:** The attention analysis methodology seems sound, and the authors provide sufficient details to understand and potentially replicate their work.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments on different models and tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed metric.
*   **New Benchmark:** The STME benchmark is a valuable resource for the community to study IVMs.
*   **Robustness to Positional Bias:** Attention accuracy is shown to be robust, making it valuable metric.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited Scope of STME:** While STME is a novel benchmark, it still consists of a relatively limited set of tasks (although diverse) and might not cover the full spectrum of potential IVMs. The generalization capabilities may be restricted by this data set's characteristics.
*   **Reliance on Attention Mechanisms:** The analysis depends heavily on the availability and interpretability of attention mechanisms, which might not be directly applicable to all MLLM architectures, especially those using different forms of attention or not using attention at all.
*   **Indirect Validation:** The paper relies on indirect validation of IVMs. While attention convergence to the target image is correlated to correct understanding, it is not a direct proof of genuine reasoning. More direct causal interventions or manipulations of the visual input would further strengthen their claims.
*   **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on *diagnosing* IVMs, not *mitigating* them. Future work could focus on how to use the attention accuracy metric to improve training and reduce IVMs.
*   **GPU limitation:** It might influence the token numbers and could limit some research directions.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper's focus on IVMs is significant, addressing a crucial limitation of existing MLLM evaluation strategies.
*   The proposed attention analysis methodology and the attention accuracy metric are novel and potentially valuable tools for understanding and improving MLLMs.
*   The STME benchmark contributes a new resource to the community.
*   However, the limited scope of STME, reliance on attention mechanisms, indirect validation, GPU limitation and lack of specific mitigation strategies reduce the overall impact. While the diagnostics are excellent, translating these diagnostics into actionable improvements to model training or architecture remains an open question. This paper opens that door and sets the stage for future research, but doesn't directly walk through it.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards a Deeper Understanding of Reasoning Capabilities in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10543v1)
- **Authors**: Annie Wong, Thomas Bäck, Aske Plaat, Niki van Stein, Anna V. Kononova
- **Abstract**: While large language models demonstrate impressive performance on static benchmarks, the true potential of large language models as self-learning and reasoning agents in dynamic environments remains unclear. This study systematically evaluates the efficacy of self-reflection, heuristic mutation, and planning as prompting techniques to test the adaptive capabilities of agents. We conduct experiments with various open-source language models in dynamic environments and find that larger models generally outperform smaller ones, but that strategic prompting can close this performance gap. Second, a too-long prompt can negatively impact smaller models on basic reactive tasks, while larger models show more robust behaviour. Third, advanced prompting techniques primarily benefit smaller models on complex games, but offer less improvement for already high-performing large language models. Yet, we find that advanced reasoning methods yield highly variable outcomes: while capable of significantly improving performance when reasoning and decision-making align, they also introduce instability and can lead to big performance drops. Compared to human performance, our findings reveal little evidence of true emergent reasoning. Instead, large language model performance exhibits persistent limitations in crucial areas such as planning, reasoning, and spatial coordination, suggesting that current-generation large language models still suffer fundamental shortcomings that may not be fully overcome through self-reflective prompting alone. Reasoning is a multi-faceted task, and while reasoning methods like Chain of thought improves multi-step reasoning on math word problems, our findings using dynamic benchmarks highlight important shortcomings in general reasoning capabilities, indicating a need to move beyond static benchmarks to capture the complexity of reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in dynamic environments. It evaluates the effectiveness of self-reflection, heuristic mutation (using an Oracle), and planning as prompting techniques. Experiments are conducted using open-source LLMs of varying sizes on the SmartPlay benchmark, which consists of diverse decision-making tasks. The study finds that larger models generally perform better, but strategic prompting can bridge the performance gap between larger and smaller models. Furthermore, advanced prompting techniques show variable outcomes, sometimes improving performance and sometimes leading to instability. The authors also demonstrate that transforming sparse rewards into dense, task-aligned quantitative rewards can improve learning effectiveness. Finally, the study finds limited evidence for emergent reasoning in dynamic tasks requiring planning and spatial coordination, highlighting limitations of current LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper offers a systematic comparison of various prompting strategies across a diverse set of tasks.  The use of SmartPlay provides a richer benchmark than many studies that focus on a single task.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The paper includes experiments isolating the impact of different modules (Reflection, Oracle, Planner), providing valuable insights into their individual contributions and interactions.
    * **Interesting Findings:** The counterintuitive finding that excessive reasoning can *harm* smaller models on simple tasks is noteworthy and warrants further investigation. The demonstration of dense reward shaping as an alternative to intensive prompt engineering is also valuable.
    * **Code Availability:** The availability of the code fosters reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Environments:** While SmartPlay is more diverse than a single-task environment, it still comprises relatively simple, text-based games. The generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world scenarios is questionable.
    * **Lack of Deep Analysis:**  While the paper presents a lot of experimental data, the *why* behind some of the observations could be explored in more detail. For instance, the reasons for the instability of advanced prompting techniques aren't deeply analyzed. Qualitative examples are provided, but a more thorough error analysis could be beneficial.
    * **Limited Novelty in Techniques:** The prompting techniques (self-reflection, evolutionary-inspired mutation) are not entirely novel. The *combination* of these techniques in the context of the chosen benchmarks constitutes a key aspect of its novelty.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * **Incrementally Novel:** The novelty lies in the *combination* and systematic evaluation of established techniques on a less-studied, dynamic benchmark. The findings regarding overthinking, the impact of dense reward shaping and performance variability contribute valuable knowledge within the field.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The study offers practical guidance for applying LLMs in dynamic environments, emphasizing the importance of prompt engineering, model size, and reward structure.

**Justification for Score**

The paper makes a solid contribution by offering a systematic evaluation of different prompting strategies in dynamic environments. The findings regarding the limitations of LLMs in planning and spatial reasoning are important reminders of their capabilities. While the techniques are not revolutionary, the comprehensive analysis and interesting findings make the paper valuable.  However, the limitations of SmartPlay benchmark in simulating real-world complexity and the depth of analysis prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work.
