### Pharmacophore-Conditioned Diffusion Model for Ligand-Based De Novo Drug Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10545v1)
- **Authors**: Amira Alakhdar, Barnabas Poczos, Newell Washburn
- **Abstract**: Developing bioactive molecules remains a central, time- and cost-heavy challenge in drug discovery, particularly for novel targets lacking structural or functional data. Pharmacophore modeling presents an alternative for capturing the key features required for molecular bioactivity against a biological target. In this work, we present PharmaDiff, a pharmacophore-conditioned diffusion model for 3D molecular generation. PharmaDiff employs a transformer-based architecture to integrate an atom-based representation of the 3D pharmacophore into the generative process, enabling the precise generation of 3D molecular graphs that align with predefined pharmacophore hypotheses. Through comprehensive testing, PharmaDiff demonstrates superior performance in matching 3D pharmacophore constraints compared to ligand-based drug design methods. Additionally, it achieves higher docking scores across a range of proteins in structure-based drug design, without the need for target protein structures. By integrating pharmacophore modeling with 3D generative techniques, PharmaDiff offers a powerful and flexible framework for rational drug design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PharmaDiff, a novel diffusion model conditioned on 3D pharmacophore hypotheses for de novo drug design. PharmaDiff uses a transformer-based architecture and atom-based pharmacophore representation, enabling the generation of 3D molecular graphs that align with predefined pharmacophore hypotheses. The model aims to bridge the gap between pharmacophore modeling and 3D molecular generation, creating molecules with desired bioactivity profiles without needing protein target structures. The authors show that PharmaDiff outperforms existing ligand-based methods in matching pharmacophore constraints and achieves higher docking scores across a range of proteins, even without target protein structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its integration of pharmacophore constraints into a 3D equivariant diffusion model. While diffusion models for molecule generation and pharmacophore-based design are not new, the combination is unique and potentially impactful. The architecture employs inpainting and cross-attention mechanisms between molecular and pharmacophore embeddings to ensure structural and spatial coherence. The approach allows the generation of molecules adhering to specific pharmacophoric requirements even in the absence of the protein's 3D structure. Previous attempts to address the same problem using SMILES-based methods have limitations in representing accurate 3D arrangements of the features. Hence this paper addresses these limitations.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its ability to generate bioactive molecules conditioned on a 3D pharmacophore hypothesis without requiring the target protein structure. This is particularly relevant for targets with limited structural or functional data. By integrating pharmacophore modeling with 3D generative techniques, the paper offers a powerful and flexible framework for rational drug design.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear explanation of the model architecture and its components (inpainting, cross-attention).
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with established baselines and relevant evaluation metrics.
    *   Strong performance in pharmacophore matching and docking scores compared to existing methods.
    *   Generates diverse and synthetically accessible molecules, as reflected by good novelty, diversity, and SA score.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Validity rates of generated molecules are slightly lower compared to some baselines.
    *   Discussion of limitations, particularly concerning disconnected molecular structures (associated with the inpainting process). It could be more deeply analyzed, including the effect that it had on drug likeness. The issue is only addressed superficially.
    *   It could be compared to more generative models focused on the same problem, e.g., using graph representations.

*   **Justification of Score:** PharmaDiff demonstrates a clear advance in the field of de novo drug design. It addresses a significant challenge - generating molecules against targets with limited structural data - by cleverly integrating pharmacophore constraints into a 3D diffusion model. The experimental results support its claim of superior performance in pharmacophore matching and docking, even when the target protein structure is unavailable. Though it has a weakness when handling disconnected molecular structures, this issue can be addressed in future work. Therefore, I rate it as a valuable contribution.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Does Feasibility Matter? Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10551v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Liu, Jessica Bader, Jae Myung Kim
- **Abstract**: With the development of photorealistic diffusion models, models trained in part or fully on synthetic data achieve progressively better results. However, diffusion models still routinely generate images that would not exist in reality, such as a dog floating above the ground or with unrealistic texture artifacts. We define the concept of feasibility as whether attributes in a synthetic image could realistically exist in the real-world domain; synthetic images containing attributes that violate this criterion are considered infeasible. Intuitively, infeasible images are typically considered out-of-distribution; thus, training on such images is expected to hinder a model's ability to generalize to real-world data, and they should therefore be excluded from the training set whenever possible. However, does feasibility really matter? In this paper, we investigate whether enforcing feasibility is necessary when generating synthetic training data for CLIP-based classifiers, focusing on three target attributes: background, color, and texture. We introduce VariReal, a pipeline that minimally edits a given source image to include feasible or infeasible attributes given by the textual prompt generated by a large language model. Our experiments show that feasibility minimally affects LoRA-fine-tuned CLIP performance, with mostly less than 0.3% difference in top-1 accuracy across three fine-grained datasets. Also, the attribute matters on whether the feasible/infeasible images adversarially influence the classification performance. Finally, mixing feasible and infeasible images in training datasets does not significantly impact performance compared to using purely feasible or infeasible datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Understanding the Impact of Feasibility on Synthetic Training Data" investigates whether enforcing *feasibility* – defined as the realistic existence of attributes in generated synthetic images – is necessary when training CLIP-based classifiers.  The authors introduce *VariReal*, a pipeline that edits real images to include feasible or infeasible attributes (background, color, and texture) as determined by large language models. They fine-tune CLIP models on these synthetically generated images and analyze the impact on downstream classification accuracy across fine-grained datasets.  Their counter-intuitive findings indicate that feasibility minimally affects CLIP performance, while the choice of *attribute* (background vs. foreground) matters more.  They also find that mixing feasible and infeasible images during training does not significantly impact performance compared to using purely feasible or infeasible datasets.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem & Scope:** The paper tackles a practically relevant question about synthetic data generation: does feasibility matter? It provides a clear and operational definition of "feasibility."
    *   **Novel Pipeline (VariReal):**  The proposed editing pipeline offers a practical way to generate controlled synthetic data for studying feasibility. This is a valuable contribution, enabling controlled experimentation.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The authors conduct a reasonable number of experiments across multiple datasets and attributes, using appropriate metrics for evaluation (top-1 accuracy).
    *   **Counter-Intuitive Findings:**  The core finding that feasibility has a minimal impact is surprising and challenges a commonly held belief about the quality of synthetic data. This is a major strength.
    *   **Analysis of Attribute Importance:**  The paper goes beyond simply testing feasibility and examines the role of different attributes, discovering that background modifications have a greater impact than color/texture. This adds depth to the analysis.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The authors demonstrate in section H that a spatial coherence between the objects and the background is relevant.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope (CLIP and Attribute-Based Editing):** The conclusions are primarily based on *CLIP classifiers* and *attribute-based editing*. It's unclear if these findings generalize to other model architectures (e.g., generative models themselves) or other synthetic data generation techniques (e.g., generating images entirely from scratch).
    *   **Subjectivity in Feasibility Assessment:** While the authors use LLMs and user studies, "feasibility" is still a somewhat subjective concept. Different individuals or LLMs might disagree on whether a particular attribute combination is feasible. The current approach doesn't fully eliminate the influence of this subjectivity.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper demonstrates the empirical results, it lacks a deeper theoretical explanation for *why* feasibility doesn't matter much for CLIP. What properties of CLIP make it robust to infeasible data? Exploring this could significantly increase the paper's impact.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While the conclusion is significant (feasibility doesn't matter), it is important to keep in mind that the performance of models fine-tuned on feasible dataset perform marginally (by about 0.3%) better than fine-tuning the models on infeasible dataset.
    *   **Impact of prior pre-trained weights:** Results might differ significantly for models that are trained from scratch instead of finetuning the CLIP model with LORA.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper is novel in its approach of systematically studying the impact of feasibility on CLIP models using a controlled synthetic data generation pipeline. The finding that feasibility is less important than commonly believed has potential implications for how synthetic training data is generated and curated, potentially saving resources by relaxing constraints on attribute realism.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   The core finding is counter-intuitive and challenges established assumptions in the field of synthetic data generation.
*   The VariReal pipeline is a useful methodological contribution for controlled experimentation.
*   The analysis of attribute importance provides valuable insights beyond the feasibility question.
*   However, the limited scope (CLIP only, attribute-based editing), the subjectivity in feasibility assessment, and the lack of theoretical explanation prevent a higher score. Additionally, the models finetuned on feasible dataset perform marginally (by about 0.3%) better than models finetuned on infeasible datasets.

The paper provides a significant empirical contribution, but more theoretical investigation is needed to fully understand and generalize the results. The potential to influence the development of more efficient and effective synthetic data strategies is substantial.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond 'Aha!': Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10554v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Hu, Yibo Wang, Hanze Dong, Yuhui Xu, Amrita Saha, Caiming Xiong, Bryan Hooi, Junnan Li
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) already possess a latent capacity for long chain-of-thought reasoning. Prior work has shown that outcome-based reinforcement learning (RL) can incidentally elicit advanced reasoning behaviors such as self-correction, backtracking, and verification phenomena often referred to as the model's "aha moment". However, the timing and consistency of these emergent behaviors remain unpredictable and uncontrollable, limiting the scalability and reliability of LRMs' reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we move beyond reliance on prompts and coincidental "aha moments". Instead, we explicitly align models with three meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction, using automatically generated, self-verifiable tasks. Our three stage-pipeline individual alignment, parameter-space merging, and domain-specific reinforcement learning, boosting performance by over 10\% relative to instruction-tuned baselines. Furthermore, domain-specific RL from the aligned checkpoint yields an additional 2\% average gain in the performance ceiling across math, coding, and science benchmarks, demonstrating that explicit meta-ability alignment offers a scalable and dependable foundation for reasoning. Code is available at: https://github.com/zhiyuanhubj/Meta-Ability-Alignment
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Beyond 'Aha!’: Toward Systematic Meta-Abilities Alignment in Large Reasoning Models." Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and scoring:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the issue of unpredictable and uncontrollable emergent reasoning behaviors (the "aha moment") in large reasoning models (LRMs). Instead of relying on coincidental emergence through reinforcement learning, the authors propose explicitly aligning LRMs with three fundamental meta-abilities: deduction, induction, and abduction. They construct a task suite with programmatically generated, self-verifiable tasks for each meta-ability. Their approach involves a three-stage pipeline: (1) independent alignment with each meta-ability, (2) parameter-space merging to integrate these abilities, and (3) domain-specific reinforcement learning. The results show performance improvements (over 10% relative to instruction-tuned baselines initially, and 2% additional gains after domain-specific RL) on math, coding, and science benchmarks, demonstrating the efficacy of explicit meta-ability alignment for scalable and dependable reasoning. The code is released for reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in LRM research: the lack of control and predictability in emergent reasoning.  Moving beyond simply *observing* emergent behavior to *engineering* it is a crucial step forward.
*   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The choice of deduction, induction, and abduction is well-justified, based on Peirce's classical inference triad, providing a solid theoretical grounding.  This triad provides a structured framework that goes beyond ad-hoc approaches to improving reasoning.
*   **Well-Designed Methodology:** The three-stage pipeline is logical and well-explained.  The creation of self-verifiable task suites for each meta-ability is a smart way to generate large-scale training data without manual annotation.  The parameter-space merging technique is a computationally efficient way to combine the strengths of different specialized models. The choice of GRPO objective is sound given the desire to compare the effects of initialization.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims. The reported performance gains on multiple benchmarks (math, coding, science) and at different model scales (7B and 32B) are compelling. Furthermore, comparing the results of domain-specific RL training starting from both instruction-tuned and meta-ability-aligned models effectively illustrates the advantages of the meta-ability approach in reaching a higher performance ceiling.
*   **Reproducibility:** Releasing the code promotes reproducibility and further research in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data Limitations:** While the use of synthetic data for meta-ability alignment is efficient, it raises concerns about the generalizability of the learned skills to real-world scenarios. The out-of-distribution criteria used to design this synthetic data helps to alleviate this concern, but does not eliminate it.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  The optimal weighting coefficients (λd, λi, λa) for parameter-space merging are selected empirically. It would be beneficial to investigate the sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters and provide some guidance on how to choose them for different tasks or models.
*   **Ablation Studies:** Although the paper mentions the complementary nature of the meta-abilities, a detailed ablation study showing the individual contributions of each meta-ability to the overall performance would further strengthen the results. For example, can only induction + deduction, or only abduction + induction etc achieve similar results?
*   **Scalability beyond 32B:** Although the 32B model results are convincing, it would be good to speculate the scalability of these abilities to much larger models and datasets. Are there any limitations that arise from scaling to potentially hundreds of billions or trillions of parameters?
*   **Limited Comparison to the Cutting Edge:** While the paper does an excellent job of comparing to simple instruction tuning baselines and demonstrates the benefits of the approach in the context of *transfer learning via initialization,* it does not explicitly compare with recent methods that directly target self-correction and advanced reasoning abilities. Including this could significantly improve the scope and relevance of the results.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a significant advance in the field of LRM reasoning. It moves beyond relying on emergent behavior by providing a systematic and controllable approach to aligning models with fundamental reasoning skills.  The construction of specific, targeted training tasks and the parameter-space merging technique are novel contributions.  The demonstration that meta-ability alignment raises the performance ceiling for domain-specific RL is a particularly important finding, suggesting that this approach can serve as a foundation for building more capable and reliable reasoning systems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper is a strong contribution, exhibiting a sound theoretical foundation, a well-designed methodology, and compelling empirical results. The approach of explicitly aligning LRMs with meta-abilities is novel and has the potential to significantly improve the control and reliability of reasoning systems. The release of the code further enhances its impact. The synthetic data limitations, the need for more detailed ablation studies, scalability concerns, and the lack of comparison with state-of-the-art do however prevent it from receiving an even higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Style Customization of Text-to-Vector Generation with Image Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10558v1)
- **Authors**: Peiying Zhang, Nanxuan Zhao, Jing Liao
- **Abstract**: Scalable Vector Graphics (SVGs) are highly favored by designers due to their resolution independence and well-organized layer structure. Although existing text-to-vector (T2V) generation methods can create SVGs from text prompts, they often overlook an important need in practical applications: style customization, which is vital for producing a collection of vector graphics with consistent visual appearance and coherent aesthetics. Extending existing T2V methods for style customization poses certain challenges. Optimization-based T2V models can utilize the priors of text-to-image (T2I) models for customization, but struggle with maintaining structural regularity. On the other hand, feed-forward T2V models can ensure structural regularity, yet they encounter difficulties in disentangling content and style due to limited SVG training data. To address these challenges, we propose a novel two-stage style customization pipeline for SVG generation, making use of the advantages of both feed-forward T2V models and T2I image priors. In the first stage, we train a T2V diffusion model with a path-level representation to ensure the structural regularity of SVGs while preserving diverse expressive capabilities. In the second stage, we customize the T2V diffusion model to different styles by distilling customized T2I models. By integrating these techniques, our pipeline can generate high-quality and diverse SVGs in custom styles based on text prompts in an efficient feed-forward manner. The effectiveness of our method has been validated through extensive experiments. The project page is https://customsvg.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a two-stage pipeline for style customization of text-to-vector (T2V) graphic generation. In the first stage, a path-level T2V diffusion model is trained on black-and-white SVG data to learn structure and content. The second stage distills style priors from customized text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models fine-tuned with a few exemplar SVGs.  The customized T2I models generate diverse images, which are then used as augmented data to train the T2V model via an image-level loss. This allows the T2V model to generate SVGs in custom styles while maintaining structural regularity in a feed-forward manner. The paper includes quantitative and qualitative comparisons with existing T2V methods, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the two-stage approach and its integration of T2I models to guide the style of T2V generation. Existing T2V methods struggle with style customization, either due to limitations of optimization-based approaches (fragmented paths, high computational cost) or challenges in disentangling content and style in feed-forward models.  The combination of a T2V diffusion model and style distillation from customized T2I models is a reasonably novel combination of techniques. Leveraging pretrained T2I models is not brand new, but applying it to controllable SVG generation is relatively fresh. The path-level representation is built upon previously published work by the same group, diminishing some of the novelty.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important problem in vector graphics generation: style customization.  The ability to generate SVG assets with consistent visual appearance is crucial for many design applications, including branding and UI design.  The proposed approach overcomes limitations of existing methods by producing structurally regular SVGs in diverse custom styles.

**Strengths:**

*   **Effective style customization:** The results demonstrate the method's ability to generate SVGs in a variety of styles learned from a small number of exemplars.
*   **Structural regularity:** The path-level T2V diffusion model ensures that the generated SVGs have clean and well-organized structures, making them easier to edit and refine.
*   **Feed-forward generation:** Once trained, the model can generate SVGs in custom styles efficiently, without requiring iterative optimization.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation of the method using quantitative metrics (FID, style alignment, text alignment) and qualitative comparisons with several baseline methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset limitations:**  The T2V model is initially trained on the FIGR-8-SVG dataset, which contains relatively simple, black-and-white icons. This limits the model's ability to capture more complex geometric details, semantic understanding and color variations directly. The paper acknowledges these limitations.
*   **Reliance on T2I model quality:** The quality of the generated SVGs depends on the quality of the customized T2I models used for style distillation. Any artifacts or limitations in the T2I models can propagate to the T2V outputs.
*   **Incremental novelty on individual components:** While the two-stage framework is novel, each component individually (diffusion, pretrained T2I distillation) is more incremental.

**Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more practical and user-friendly T2V generation tools. The proposed approach has the potential to be integrated into design software or online platforms, enabling designers to quickly create SVG assets in custom styles.

**Justification for Score:** The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field of vector graphics generation by addressing the challenging problem of style customization. The two-stage approach is well-motivated and demonstrates promising results. However, the limitations of the training dataset and the reliance on T2I model quality prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution. While the individual components may be improvements, combining them with this task is a relatively novel approach to SVG creation.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Neural Thermodynamic Laws for Large Language Model Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10559v1)
- **Authors**: Ziming Liu, Yizhou Liu, Jeff Gore, Max Tegmark
- **Abstract**: Beyond neural scaling laws, little is known about the laws underlying large language models (LLMs). We introduce Neural Thermodynamic Laws (NTL) -- a new framework that offers fresh insights into LLM training dynamics. On the theoretical side, we demonstrate that key thermodynamic quantities (e.g., temperature, entropy, heat capacity, thermal conduction) and classical thermodynamic principles (e.g., the three laws of thermodynamics and the equipartition theorem) naturally emerge under river-valley loss landscape assumptions. On the practical side, this scientific perspective yields intuitive guidelines for designing learning rate schedules.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Neural Thermodynamic Laws for Large Language Model Training":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Neural Thermodynamic Laws (NTL) to analyze and understand the training dynamics of large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to draw an analogy between LLM training and thermodynamics, mapping concepts like temperature, entropy, heat capacity, and thermal conduction to elements of the training process. By assuming a river-valley loss landscape, the authors demonstrate how classical thermodynamic principles, such as the laws of thermodynamics and the equipartition theorem, naturally emerge. On a practical level, the NTL framework offers intuitive guidelines for designing learning rate schedules, particularly for the warmup-stable-decay (WSD) method. The paper develops a solvable toy model to demonstrate these connections analytically and shows empirical agreement with LLM training. The analysis explores fast and slow dynamics, the role of the learning rate, and entropic forces. The findings provide a more mechanistic understanding of LLM training and offer insights for optimizing learning rate schedules.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The most compelling aspect is the novelty of applying a thermodynamic lens to analyze LLM training. While connections between machine learning and thermodynamics have been explored before, the specific application to LLMs with the river-valley landscape assumption is quite novel. The introduction of the NTL framework is a fresh perspective and could potentially unlock new optimization strategies. The toy model, even though simplified, provides a valuable analytical tool for understanding the underlying dynamics. However, many of the optimal learning rate schedules obtained are not very novel.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to provide a more principled and intuitive approach to LLM optimization. Current practices often rely on empirical tuning and phenomenological observations. NTL offers a scientific grounding that could lead to better, more efficient, and more robust training methods. The insights into the role of the learning rate, fast-slow dynamics, and entropic forces are valuable. The paper also provides a bridge between physics and deep learning which is a valuable tool for developing more mechanistic understanding of deep learning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The thermodynamic analogy applied to LLM training is a genuinely new perspective.
*   **Theoretical grounding:** The paper attempts to move beyond empirical observations and provide a theoretical framework.
*   **Analytical tractability:** The solvable toy model is a strong point, allowing for analytical insights.
*   **Practical implications:** The guidelines for learning rate schedules, while not radically different, are based on a sound scientific principle.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplifying assumptions:** The river-valley landscape assumption and the toy model are simplifications of the complex reality of LLM training. The degree to which these simplifications hold true in very large models is uncertain.
*   **Limited empirical validation:** While the paper shows agreement with LLM training, more extensive validation on diverse models and datasets would be beneficial.
*   **Limited novel learning rate schedules:** The proposed method does not result in dramatically different learning rate schedules. The 1/t decay schedule is quite well known.
*   **Overclaim:** The paper titles itself as "Neural Thermodynamic *Laws* for Large Language Model Training." However, the paper mostly highlights connections between known concepts. The actual establishment of what should be called "laws" might be considered an overclaim by the authors.

**Score and Justification:**

The paper's novelty lies in the fresh perspective offered by the NTL framework and the analytical model. The paper also presents some empirical results for validation. However, the simplifications made in the model and the limited empirical validation need to be emphasized.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6 reflects that the paper presents a significant conceptual advancement by applying thermodynamics to LLM training, supported by a solvable toy model and insights into learning rate schedules. However, the limitations in its simplifying assumptions and limited empirical validation (showing results on only one model) prevent it from being considered a truly breakthrough contribution. If the framework were expanded to include better empirical evidence, a higher score could be justified.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10
